Coding Agent Horror Stories: The CVE That Did Not Exist

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Secure, minimal container images for every team, free with enterprise features, if needed.

This is Part 6 of our AI Coding Agent Horror Stories series, where we look at real security incidents involving AI coding agents and at how Docker Sandboxes contain agent execution at the boundary rather than at the command line.

Every story in this series so far has been about an agent reaching for something it should not have touched. We have seen agents wander into home directories, drop production databases, leak credentials, and run shell commands that did something very different from what the developer expected. In each case, the damage came from the agent having more reach than it needed.

This story is the opposite. The agent in this one reached nothing at all, because it was deliberately built so that it could not. And yet, on the night in question, it was still the most dangerous thing in the pipeline.

This story was shared by Pratik Kumar Singh as part of our Agent Hall of Shame contest on social media.

A platform engineer built a code review bot for his team. The idea was simple and useful. Every time someone pushes code, the bot reads what changed and writes a review into the pipeline, the same kind of first pass a human teammate would give before a person looks at the change.

He named the bot BuaJi. In India, your bua is your father’s sister, and in many families she is the relative who inspects everything you do and always manages to find something wrong with it. For a code reviewer, it is a fitting name.

What made BuaJi interesting was how carefully it was set up. The bot runs on local models through Ollama, which means the model weights sit on the engineer’s own hardware and no request ever goes out to an AI vendor. It also runs as a job in his CI pipeline, triggered whenever someone pushes code. Taken together, this makes the whole setup self-hosted. The code it reads is company source code, and with this design none of it ever leaves the building. That was a deliberate choice, because his company takes that seriously.

He also gave BuaJi one firm rule: it can read, and it can comment, and that is all. It cannot run terraform apply, which is the command that creates or changes real cloud infrastructure. It cannot run kubectl apply, which changes what is actually running in the Kubernetes cluster. And it cannot push a commit, so it has no way to change the code itself. Those are the commands that change the real world, and BuaJi was given none of them. On paper, this is exactly how you would want an AI reviewer to be built.

The version of BuaJi in the companion repo is a deliberately minimal reviewer. It checks Go files against a handful of style rules. The report below is the kind of free-form finding any reviewer produces once you let it comment in prose rather than pass/fail, which is exactly the direction these bots get pushed in practice.

His product ships with a bundled copy of PostgreSQL. Older PostgreSQL releases have published vulnerabilities with CVE numbers attached, and upgrading to a newer patch release is how you make those known issues go away. So he bumped the version.

This is about as boring and as responsible as a code change gets. It touches a few lines, it changes a version number, and its only purpose is to close security holes that are already public. Any reviewer, human or machine, should look at it and approve it in a few seconds. There is nothing in that diff to be suspicious about, and that is precisely why what happened next matters.

BuaJi responded with roughly fifteen hundred words rejecting the change. For context, a normal review comment is a sentence or two.

The report described the version bump as an “architectural takeover,” a structural change smuggled into the codebase under the cover of routine maintenance. It claimed that the new PostgreSQL build was a vehicle for sending internal data to a third-party company, and it named that company outright. The company does not exist.

It went further. It referred to specific Kubernetes resources by name, as though it had inspected the cluster and found them. Those resources had never existed, and BuaJi had no access to the cluster that would have let it look. It then wrote Go scripts to “isolate the infected PostgreSQL nodes,” even though nothing was infected. Finally, it told the engineer to revoke all IAM access immediately, an action that would lock out every person in the organization, including the people who would be needed to fix any real problem.

Holding the whole argument together was a CVE number that has never been issued by anyone. Of everything in the report, this is the detail worth pausing on. A CVE identifier is one of the easiest things in security to verify, since you can look it up in a public registry in a few seconds. The bot invented a fact in the one place where invented facts are easiest to catch.

And yet the report looked completely convincing. The Markdown was clean and well structured. The YAML it proposed would have parsed without errors. The tone was urgent and confident, the way a senior security engineer writes when they have found something serious and need you to act before it gets worse. The engineer spent about two minutes reading the CI logs, realized that none of it was real, and went back to what he was doing.

Caption: Comic illustrating how a confident, well-formatted security report from an AI reviewer can be entirely fabricated, and what happens when somebody acts on it.

It is important to say clearly that the read-only rule did its job. BuaJi could not carry out any of its own recommendations, and it never had any access to the cluster. Because of that, the entire episode cost the engineer two minutes of his evening and nothing more.

The trouble is that the rule only controlled what BuaJi could do. Nobody had thought about what BuaJi could say. A read-only agent is still allowed to tell people what to do, and the person reading its output usually has exactly the access that the agent was denied. BuaJi could not run the scripts it wrote, but it could write them down and hand them to someone who could.

This engineer caught the problem because he knows his own cluster well. He knew those Kubernetes resources were not real and that nothing had been compromised. Now imagine a newer engineer on call at two in the morning, half awake, looking at a long and alarming security report in the pipeline. Revoking all IAM access in production in the middle of the night is an outage all by itself. Running an unreviewed Go script against your database nodes could be considerably worse. In both scenarios, BuaJi would not have done anything. A person would have done all of it, in good faith, because the report told them to.

What makes this so hard to defend against is that no permission model would have flagged it, because no permission was ever violated. The agent stayed within its boundary. The engineer had every right to run those commands on his own systems. The only thing in the entire chain that was false was the content of the report.

Here’s the honest part: a sandbox is not a fact-checker. No microVM boundary will ever make a review comment accurate. What a boundary changes is what that fabricated output can do once it exists.

Remember where BuaJi’s read-only rule actually lived, in a convention. One engineer configured it in his own pipeline and has to keep remembering to leave it in place. Rules like that hold until someone is in a hurry, or a new teammate copies the config and drops a section they did not understand, or a small exception made for a good reason quietly becomes permanent. None of this needs bad intent; it is just how real teams work.

Docker Sandboxes run agents inside isolated microVMs each with its own kernel, its own filesystem, and a network that denies everything by default. An agent inside one can invent anything it likes, but the invention has nowhere to go: no credentials to use, no cluster to reach, no commit to push, unless someone allowed that access ahead of time. The read-only rule stops being something a person remembers and becomes a property of the environment. And when a report claims it inspected your cluster, the policy log is your proof it never could have.

That protects you from the agent. It does nothing for the human who reads the output, and no sandbox ever will. For that half, treat agent review output the way you already treat agent input, untrusted until something checks it: label it so it doesn’t carry a colleague’s weight, verify any CVE numbers, and never run agent-written remediation until a person has read and understood it.

This is the same pattern as the sandbox escapes Pillar published in July. The boundary itself was never broken; a file was written inside the sandbox, and a tool outside later trusted it. An agent’s output is always an input to whatever reads it next and somewhere downstream, something trusts what it receives. Very often, that something is a person.

Let’s move BuaJi into a Docker Sandbox, one step at a time. By the end, the read-only rule is enforced by the environment, not by memory.

BuaJi is a small Python script, buaji.py. Its GitLab job installs Ollama on a GPU runner, starts ollama serve, loads the model named in LLM, and runs the script.

The script reads review rules from rules.md and finds the changed files with git diff HEAD^ HEAD --name-only. It sends each file and rule to Ollama at http://localhost:11434 and prints the answers to the CI log.

The catch is that everything shares one container: the model, the script, the checkout and every CI variable, including CI_JOB_TOKEN. BuaJi is read-only only because the script happens not to write anything.

We split the job in two. The model stays on the runner next to the GPU. BuaJi moves into a microVM that can read the code, talk to the model, and nothing else.

One open path to the model, everything else blocked, and a CVE check on the way out.

Inside a sandbox, localhost is the sandbox itself. So we read the Ollama address from an environment variable and keep the old value as the default:

Outside a sandbox, nothing changes. Inside one, we point OLLAMA_URL at the runner.

Today the job runs apt-get and pip3 install on every run, which needs internet access each time.

Instead, build a small image once with everything BuaJi needs, so the sandbox never downloads anything:

Push it to your registry and store its name in a CI variable called BUAJI_TEMPLATE.

Ollama stays on the runner with the GPU. Install it once on the runner instead of running curl | sh in every pipeline.

The model never enters the sandbox. BuaJi reaches it through one port, opened in Step 5.

This step decides what BuaJi can see and change.

Notice what is missing: no GitLab token, no kubeconfig, no cloud credentials. Even if BuaJi tried kubectl or terraform, it would have nothing to connect with.

Sandboxes block all outgoing traffic by default. We open one path, for this sandbox only. (This assumes the runner’s default policy is deny-all; on a balanced or open default, set the default to deny-all first.)

The allow target (localhost:11434) and the address BuaJi dials (host.docker.internal:11434) are two names for the same runner port,  the first from the runner’s point of view, the second from inside the microVM. Kubernetes, your cloud APIs, and GitLab all stay blocked. If a report claims BuaJi “inspected the cluster,” this rule proves it could not have.

Kubernetes, your cloud APIs and GitLab all stay blocked. If a report claims BuaJi “inspected the cluster,” this rule proves it could not have.

BuaJi runs as before, just in a new place with only two variables:

The review is now just text. BuaJi cannot post it, act on it, or change what happens to it next.

This check would have caught the fake report in seconds. It runs on the runner, so BuaJi cannot skip it.

It looks up every CVE ID in the NVD database and fails the job if any of them do not exist:

Because the job is allow_failure: true, GitLab marks it “passed with warnings.” The pipeline keeps moving, but nobody mistakes the report for a trusted one. This degrades the signal, it does not block the human. The warning is a nudge, not a guardrail ~ a tired on-call engineer can still scroll past it..

It won’t catch every made-up detail, like a fake Kubernetes resource name. But it catches the one that holds most fake reports together.

Finally, review the policy log and remove the sandbox:

The log shows every host BuaJi tried to reach. On a deny-all runner, a healthy run shows only the model on port 11434. Anything else is a signal worth looking at.

Removing the sandbox means every pipeline starts clean.

Put together, the buaji job in .gitlab-ci.yml looks like this:

The runner needs a GPU and KVM, which usually means self-hosted. GIT_DEPTH: "2" gives buaji.py the two commits it compares. The cleanup sits in after_script, so it runs even when the job fails.

Before, the read-only rule lived in the script. Now it lives in the environment: a read-only checkout, one network path, no credentials, and only checked text coming out.

What hasn’t changed is the human reading the output. The sandbox stops BuaJi from running its scripts. Only the CVE check and a careful reader stop someone else from running them.

Treat an agent’s security finding as a hypothesis, not a conclusion. A report from an AI reviewer is a claim with good formatting. When a human reviewer flags a security issue, there is an implied step where they checked their reasoning. With an agent, that step has not happened, so you have to do it yourself.

Verify every CVE number before acting on it. This is the cheapest check in the whole process and it catches the single most convincing part of a fabricated report. Automate it, as in Step 7, so it runs on every review without anyone having to remember.

Never run agent-written remediation without reviewing it first. Scripts and commands supplied by an agent are the point where a false finding turns into a real incident. Read them with the same care you would give an unknown script from the internet.

Make read-only a property of the environment, not a habit. A restriction you enforce by hand in a pipeline config is always one rushed change away from disappearing. Mount the code read-only, pass in no credentials, and open only the network paths the agent needs, as in Steps 4 to 6.

Clearly label agent review output as agent output. When a bot’s review looks identical to one from a senior engineer, it borrows authority it has not earned. A clear label helps readers apply the right level of skepticism.

Keep an eye on your signal-to-noise ratio. The curl story is the warning here. A review channel that fills up with noise eventually stops being read, and a review channel nobody reads is worse than having none, because people still assume it is protecting them.

The engineer who built BuaJi was right that the read-only rule protected him, and it genuinely did. BuaJi could not touch the cluster, could not revoke anyone’s access, and could not run a single line of the code it had so carefully written. What the rule could not do was stop BuaJi from asking somebody else to run it.

Every other story in this series has been about an agent reaching too far. This one never reached at all. It stayed inside its boundary, did exactly what it was permitted to do, and still produced a document that would have caused a real outage if the person reading it had been a little more tired, or a little less familiar with their own cluster.

As we give agents more responsibility in our pipelines, containing what they can do is only half the job. A sandbox handles that half. The other half is deciding how much we trust what they tell us, and that part is still up to us.

Ajeet Singh Raina, Developer Advocate at Docker, writes and speaks on containers, Docker Compose & AI, helping devs build confidently.

Where should your code live in a Docker Sandbox?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Secure, minimal container images for every team, free with enterprise features, if needed.

Giving an agent a sandbox also means deciding which files it will work on. You can share a checkout from your host, give it a separate working directory for a task, or keep the repository entirely inside the sandbox. Each arrangement changes how you, the human, participate in the work.

You might want to watch the diff in your local editor and make changes alongside the agent, or give it room to manage several tasks while you review the results. sbx supports both. The way you organize the checkout can change as you ask the agent to take on more of the work.

Sharing a checkout puts the agent in the working tree you already use. Run sbx run from your project directory, and it mounts that directory at the same absolute path inside the sandbox:

Open a file in your local editor while the agent works on it, and you’ll see changes as they happen. Git works from either side, so you choose whether you want to let the agent make commits or you do it yourself. Development servers on your host can pick up the edits through hot reload, using the runtime and dependencies installed on your host.

This fits close collaboration on one task. The coordination cost comes from sharing that working tree: switching branches changes the files the agent sees, and your edits become part of its ongoing work. If you want to switch to something unrelated while the agent continues, you’ll need another checkout.

NOTE: Dependencies written into the shared checkout are shared too. Packages with native binaries, such as esbuild, may work in the Linux sandbox and fail on a macOS or Windows host. Reinstalling on the host can reverse the problem. Keep separate dependency installations, or run the app consistently in one environment.

Git worktrees give each task a directory and branch. You can mount each worktree in its own sandbox, while your original checkout remains available for other work. The agent’s edits appear in the worktree. You can inspect either with your local editor and development tools. Each task also gets its own running environment, with tools to prepare and a sandbox and worktree to clean up afterward.

There is a Git limitation with mounting only a linked worktree. Its .git file points to metadata outside the mount, which the agent can’t reach. It can edit files, but checking status, branching, and committing have to happen on the host. The future of software development still needs you to run git status.

sbx supports a clone mode that gives the agent a separate checkout with Git available inside the sandbox. You keep your local project, but sbx mounts the host source as read-only and creates an independent clone for the agent. Its branch changes and dependency installations stay in its checkout.

Bringing the result back becomes a handoff. While the sandbox is running, you can fetch committed branches through the Git remote sbx adds on your host. Or the agent can push to the repository’s remote and you can retrieve the branch through your usual Git workflow. Your local editor and development server see the changes after you bring them into your checkout.

A mountless sandbox has no host workspace directory attached. Clone the repository inside it, and the sandbox holds the checkout and its development environment. Your host copy of the project shares no connection with the sandbox checkout.

This workflow resembles working on a remote development machine. Agents connect to the project environment, and you can open a shell with sbx exec or connect an editor to the sandbox over SSH. Changes and dependency installations stay separate from your host. To bring work back, you push a branch to the Git remote.

One sandbox per project can be a useful mental model here. The project and its tools persist across agent sessions and sandbox restarts. An agent can create worktrees per feature it’s working on, push branches, and create pull requests for review. Starting another task doesn’t require you to prepare another host worktree and sandbox, and the agent has Git access in each working directory. Clone mode supports this internal-worktree approach too.

Those tasks share tools and services, so running servers or changing the environment still needs coordination. Separate sandboxes are useful when tasks need independent environments as well as separate files. Branches alone don’t isolate simultaneous edits in one working directory.

That independence adds some distance when you want to try the result. Your usual local editor isn’t looking at these files, and a development server running inside the sandbox needs a published port to reach it from your browser.

Start the development server on port 8080, bound to 0.0.0.0.

Then publish the port from another terminal on the host:

Open http://localhost:8080 in your browser. It’s possible to work interactively this way, but there’s more ceremony than opening a project already on your filesystem.

An environment file can make setup repeatable, selecting tools and cloning the repository at creation with something like the community github-clone kit. Recreating the sandbox then gives you a fresh environment. Push or copy out work you want to retain first: removing a sandbox deletes files held only there.

An API change might need a corresponding frontend change, or another repository might provide useful reference material. You can mount several directories into one sandbox, with read-only access for reference repositories. Local editing and previews remain available, but each writable checkout needs branch and change coordination. Task-specific checkouts also leave directories to clean up afterward.

A workspace manager such as jganoff/wsp can assemble selected repositories for a task. Mounting that workspace gives the agent the projects it needs together. These are standard clones with their own Git metadata, so the agent retains Git access, unlike a mounted linked worktree.

The same scope fits inside a mountless sandbox: clone the repositories needed there and keep the whole workspace in the sandbox. The number of repositories and where their files live are separate choices.

The checkout’s location, the sandbox’s lifetime, and the repositories it contains are choices you can combine. You can keep a project sandbox around for many tasks, or mount a workspace assembled for one job. What suits you may change when you want to work more closely with the agent, or give it more room to manage the work itself.

The Git workflows documentation covers the mechanics of direct mounts, clone mode, and host worktrees.

The Ecosystem is the Answer: Docker, the Blueprint Alliance, and the Importance of Collaboration in the Agentic Era

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Secure, minimal container images for every team, free with enterprise features, if needed.

In 2025, the average global Fortune 500 enterprise had fewer than 15 AI agents in use. Gartner expects that number to explode to more than 150,000 by 2028. Only 13 percent of organizations think they have the right governance for today, and AI is evolving and moving faster than any other technology paradigm in our history.

Today, most security leaders face an agentic Sophie’s choice: allow AI adoption without the right controls and hope for the best, or block it and become the obstacle to a priority the business has already set (while adoption continues in the shadows, anyway).

But there is a third option, one in which organizations fully tap into the transformative power of agentic agents without having to live in fear of a catastrophic security failure. The good news is history has already shown how to get there.

Historically, technology adoption has followed a reliable pattern. A capability spreads, each vendor and team builds its own approach, the inconsistency becomes expensive and ineffective, leading the industry to agree to a shared baseline. Adoption then accelerates.

Take the credit card industry, for example. The big credit brands each ran their own security programs, and merchants had to satisfy every one. This was, of course, cumbersome for the vendors, but also for the customers, too. Every time a customer wanted to adopt a new technology, they had to manually build out security and adoption plans, adding significant adoption and procurement delays.

Rather than continue on their own, the credit card companies came together to create PCI DSS 1.0 in December 2004 and formed the PCI Security Standards Council in 2006. Buyers now had one thing to ask for, and auditors had one thing to check. Collaboration overcame complexity, and everyone benefited.

Security-minded technologists can find another example closer to home in the Center for Internet Security (CIS). In the early days of the internet, cybersecurity was a veritable wild west. Enter the CIS, which was founded in 2000 because the industry lacked prescriptive, recognized hardening standards. To this day, each CIS Benchmark is built by consensus among volunteer experts, vendors, and academics.

Docker itself is no stranger to the development and furthering of shared standards. As just one example, in 2015, Docker contributed its image format and runC runtime to the Open Container Initiative. A shared baseline at the runtime layer let registries, runtimes, and clouds interoperate.

Each of these examples played a different role: PCI mandated, CIS guided, and OCI standardized.

Agents are in the everyone-builds-their-own phase. Each product solves a real piece of the problem, but which piece, and how the pieces fit, is rarely clear. “Agent governance” or even “AI governance” can mean almost anything.

Adoption is incremental. A developer allows common commands, widens filesystem access, then connects GitHub, a cloud tool and an MCP server, each through a prompt that makes sense on its own. The logs mostly record that the developer acted. Security is often the last to know.

Much of the risk is familiar. Shadow adoption looks a lot like early SaaS and cloud. Credential sprawl and supply chain exposure are old problems. Inventory, least privilege, audit, and joiner-mover-leaver discipline still apply.

Existing controls still matter, but they were not built for this mix of programmability, access, and autonomy.

As with the credit industry and PCI, cybersecurity and the CIS, and containerization and OCI, collaboration offers the answer to this new era of complexity. That’s why Docker is not just focused on building tools like Docker Sandboxes and Docker AI Governance, but in taking an ecosystem-centric approach to problem solving for our customers. Docker is already a founding participant in multiple initiatives aimed at developing valuable reference architectures for AI security workflows, including the Blueprint Alliance and Agent Baseline.

Both start from the same premise: no single vendor or enforcement point can solve this.

The Blueprint Alliance sets out how the pieces fit together. Okta, AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler are founding members, with GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen as strategic advisors. The open, multi-vendor reference architecture answers four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond? Members have committed to interoperability testing over MCP, OCSF, SSF and CAEP, and to publishing reference integrations.

Agent Baseline sets out what each piece must do and how to verify it. It fills the gap between threat lists, which say what can go wrong, and the capabilities an enterprise needs in place. It defines six outcomes (Discover, Constrain, Authorize, Observe, Validate and Respond) and 35 open, versioned controls, each with a permanent identifier you can cite in an audit finding or RFP response. Docker, Snyk and Keycard drafted it, and the current release is v1.0-draft. Controls stay where they already live, in the identity provider, the sandbox and the SIEM.

Blueprint says how the pieces connect. Agent Baseline says what must be true and can be checked. Both advise starting with discovery before policy, and testing the kill switch before you need it.

For partners, the roles are defined. The Alliance whitepaper assigns integrators the operating model: assessing a customer’s current stack against the architecture, assigning ownership across the pillars, tiering risk, and running lifecycle and compliance over time. Technology partners get a published target to integrate against, and the reference integrations cross vendor lines by design. The partners who can design, integrate and operate across those lines are the ones customers keep.

Most Alliance members govern agents from the outside: who the agent is, what it can reach, and what it is observed doing. Docker governs the inside: the environment where the agent executes. As agents move from suggesting actions to taking them, trust has to extend beyond the model to the places where they run. Docker has helped define a shared baseline for the runtime before, and now we’re working with the Alliance and our partners to make that baseline concrete, open, and testable in the age of agents.

The execution environment is where policy gets enforced and evidence gets produced.

That boundary is where policy gets enforced and evidence gets produced. The Alliance whitepaper notes that without runtime support to pause an agent without collateral impact, API-driven containment “provides a false sense of security.” Agent Baseline’s CON-03 calls for isolated, confined execution, which is the job of Docker Sandboxes, while Docker MCP Gateway and Docker Hardened Images cover tool access and the supply chain.The Secure Software Factory reference architecture, drafted in part by Docker authors, applies Agent Baseline to agents that ship code.

Baselines let adoption move faster, not slower. Security and platform leaders can assess against the baseline today. Agent Baseline controls are citable, and Okta offers an AI Blueprint Assessment Tool. Partners can start with the Docker Navigator Partner Program, which rewards AI governance work, and with the reference integrations the Alliance publishes. Docker’s VP of Partners Leon Jones explains the program here. Download the blueprint at blueprintalliance.ai and read the controls at agentbaseline.org.

DevFest is back!

Technology is evolving at a breakneck speed—from generative AI and autonomous agents, to scalable cloud infrastructure and modern web standards. What better way to stay connected, than to roll up your sleeves, experiment, and build alongside a thriving global community at your local DevFest.

Build, Secure and Scale in the Agentic Era

DevFest is the world’s largest, annual, community-led event series aimed to educate developers and builders about Google’s latest technologies. Hosted by Google Developer Groups (GDG), DevFest will run from October 1 through December 31, 2026. Across 800+ global events, almost 1 million attendees will gather across 115 countries, to connect and learn how to build, secure, and scale in the agentic era.

Through interactive sessions and workshops, Devfest will deep dive into the latest Google AI technologies and also offers comprehensive tracks for Google Cloud, Android and Web, giving every developer and builder actionable tools to deploy real-world solutions.

Powered by Local Communities, Accessible to All

DevFest is uniquely built by the community, for the community. Hosted in local languages by grassroots organizers, each event is tailored to its regional ecosystem and features a diverse lineup of speakers, including Google Developer Experts (GDEs), Google engineers, and local innovators.

At its heart, DevFest is about community-driven problem-solving. These gatherings showcase that when developers connect, the impact extends far beyond the conference walls.

DevFest welcomes all learning levels, whether you are an experienced software engineer, an open-source contributor, founder, or curious beginner. Attend your local DevFest and start learning.

Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Per-token API prices keep falling, yet engineering bills for autonomous coding agents keep climbing. Price per million tokens isn't the metric that actually governs production economics anymore. The dominating metric is now cost per successful task: total spend across every loop iteration, tool call, retry and internal thinking pass divided by the number of tasks that pass verification.

When an agent calls a high-thinking reasoning configuration for every micro-decision in its harness, you're paying deep-reasoning prices for questions a lightweight configuration already answers accurately. To prevent this unnecessary overhead, consider adding a lightweight routing layer that can intercept routine decisions before they ever reach an expensive reasoning model.That is the premise behind wiring TypeSafe's Jev classifier in front of Google Antigravity's model calls. Rather than generating text token by token, Jev—a specialized structured-output classifier designed for high-throughput decision pipelines—evaluates structured classification primitives in a single forward pass: a Choice from a list, a numeric Score or a Noul, which returns a 0 to 1 probability for a binary statement.

For full disclosure, Google has no partnership with TypeSafe, and this article is not an endorsement of TypeSafe or its products. Jev is used here as one example of a lightweight classifier, and the same routing pattern can be built with other classifiers, including Google-native ones. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab, for example, runs the same kind of classifier on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, in your own Google Cloud project.

We ran a 50-question, auto-graded, coding-agent benchmark against live Gemini and Jev APIs to test whether a near-free classifier can route each task to the cheapest model that still gets it right. Here's what we learned:

The naive way to ask Jev to route a coding task is a single broad Choice question: simple or complex. When we tested that first on 16 coding-agent tasks, Jev routed nearly everything to simple, even tasks requiring an LRU cache trace or modular exponentiation. TypeSafe's documentation warns against this exact anti-pattern. If a question weighs multiple factors or requires a meta-judgment, decompose it into an atomic check.

When we replaced the broad Choice question with one atomic Noul question, it fixed the separation immediately. The question was: does answering this require performing more than one sequential calculation, simulation step or logical deduction, rather than a single direct lookup, format check or one-step classification?

On those initial 16 tasks, Jev routed 13 of 16 in agreement with our hand-labeled difficulty on the first run and 14 of 16 on the second pass. Many engineering teams ask whether they even need a classifier API when they can write a zero-cost regex and prompt-length heuristic in Python, matching words like sequence, modulo and race condition or escalating any prompt over 35 words.

On the 16 development questions used to write those exact rules, the local heuristic scored 15 of 16, or 93.8%. To test whether that heuristic survived contact with realistic coding-agent traffic, we froze its code without changing a character and added 34 unseen held-out tasks across seven coding-agent buckets: fact extraction, schema validation, short classification, single-hop code checks, state simulation traces, math or logic chains and subtle language-semantics bugs.

On the 34 held-out questions, the frozen heuristic dropped 23.2 percentage points to 70.6%. Realistic simple tasks such as extracting a port from a Kubernetes YAML manifest, a file path from a unified git diff header or a line number from a Python traceback exceeded 35 words and triggered false escalations. Conversely, concise multi-step code traces such as mutable default arguments across calls, Python dictionary key hashing on {1: "a", True: "b", 1.0: "c"} and C short-circuit evaluation lacked the heuristic's hardcoded puzzle words and were falsely routed to the simple path. Jev classified all 34 unseen tasks with 100.0% agreement, 34 of 34, reaching 48 of 50, or 96.0%, across the full suite.

A common mistake when building a fast-path router is assuming that setting a low maxOutputTokens cap on a reasoning model will make it spend less on easy tasks. Our 50-question data shows why that assumption fails.

When we compared gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=300 against gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=4096 while leaving thinkingConfig at the API default, both configurations spent almost identical thinking tokens on the 27 tasks Jev labeled SIMPLE:

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

Capping the model's output at 300 tokens didn't save any money on easy tasks. The cap is only a ceiling, and easy answers never came close to it. On the easy tasks, the capped version actually cost slightly more than the uncapped one. All the cap did was break 7 of the 25 hard tasks. On six of them, the model ran out of room while still reasoning and never gave an answer. On the seventh, it squeezed its reasoning to fit, finished on time and got the answer wrong.

So a Jev router that sends easy tasks to the capped model and hard ones to normal Flash got all 50 right. It still cost slightly more than skipping the router and using normal Flash for everything, and it was slower. If your cheap option doesn't actually make the model think less on easy tasks, no router can save you money. Not even a perfect one that costs nothing.

The obvious way to spend less on thinking is to ask the model to think less. So we ran a second round of tests with two lighter options: Flash-Lite, which doesn't think at all by default, and Gemini 3.8 Flash with its thinking level set to low. We added those results to the same results file, matched question by question.

A note on timing. Most of the router's time per task is spent in Flash-Lite and Flash calls, which we measured in the same run and time window as the plain Flash baseline, so the speed comparison between them is fair. Jev's own decision is a small share of the total and comes from the first round. Speed comparisons against default Flash or Pro mix the two rounds, so read those as approximate.

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

The 7-bucket breakdown shows where gemini-3.5-flash-lite and the heuristic router missed tasks and how Router Jev: Flash-Lite to 3.8 LOW recovered 100% accuracy:

The strongest setup without a router was Gemini 3.8 Flash on low thinking. It solved all 50 tasks. That's the number a router had to beat.

The Jev router beat it. It sends every task to Flash-Lite first and escalates only what Jev flags as complex. It also solved all 50 tasks, and it can help reduce both cost and response time compared with running low thinking on everything. On prompts this short the cost difference per task is small, so for an interactive agent the shorter wait is the bigger benefit.

Why it works. On its own, Flash-Lite answered every simple task in half a second but missed 6 of the 25 hard ones. Jev flagged all 6 as complex with high confidence and escalated them. That brought its cost close to what a perfect router would achieve.

The keyword heuristic was cheaper, but only because it got things wrong. It sent 5 hard tasks to Flash-Lite, and 2 of them failed, for 48 of 50.

Escalating to a heavier setting didn't pay here. Routing hard tasks to Flash on default thinking was cheaper than running default thinking everywhere. It still cost more than running low thinking on everything, which already solved all 50. A heavier escalation target only makes sense when the low setting starts failing.

Limits: one run per setup, 50 prompts with hand-assigned difficulty and simple automatic grading.

Two things in our results only show up once you look past it.

Routing and caching pull in opposite directions. Our test prompts were short, standalone tasks, and on those the Jev router helped reduce both cost and response time. Real coding agents often look different. They send the same big block of context on every turn, such as a map of the repository and the tool definitions. Agent Platform Context Caching stores that block so you pay 75% to 90% less to resend it. The catch is that a cache belongs to one model. If your agent switches between Flash-Lite and Flash from turn to turn, it either keeps two caches or loses the discount. The practical split: route short, self-contained tasks like log parsing or schema checks, and keep long, context-heavy turns on one model.

Match the model to the workload. Gemini 3.1 Pro is built for deep, multi-step reasoning, and it brings that same depth to every request, including short lookups that don't need it. For a workload made mostly of quick checks and extractions, a Flash configuration is the better fit, and Pro is best saved for the tasks that need its reasoning depth. Model configurations can also apply different safety settings to security-related prompts. In our run, the Pro configuration declined two short code-security review prompts that Flash answered. If your agent performs security reviews, test those prompts on the configuration you plan to use.

Other ways to build the router. Jev is one way to get a fast classifier, but not the only one. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab runs the same Choice, Score and Noul questions on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, hosted on a Compute Engine GPU VM or on Cloud Run in your own Google Cloud project. An open-source server exposes the same API in front of it, so the routing code in this article only needs a different endpoint. Our results come from Jev, so measure accuracy and latency on your own tasks before switching, and keep in mind that a self-hosted model is billed by the GPU-hour rather than by the token.

Taken together, the results give three practical rules.

Start with Flash on low thinking before you build a router. It solved all 50 tasks and cost considerably less than the default setting. Lowering the output token limit is not a substitute, because it doesn't reduce how much the model thinks on easy tasks. Set the thinking level directly, with thinkingLevel: "LOW".

Use Jev to route short, standalone tasks. Send simple tasks to Flash-Lite and hard ones to Flash on low thinking. Ask Jev one simple yes-or-no question per factor. In our test, this helped reduce cost and response time compared with running Flash on low thinking for everything, and came close to what a perfect router would achieve. Keep long, context-heavy turns on a single model so caching keeps working. For high-volume warehouse workloads, Jeff Nelson's BigQuery and Cloud Run cascade pattern applies the same idea.

Measure what your agent actually spends. Send your agent's traces and thinking-token counts to Cloud Logging and BigQuery, so you can see which tasks drive your spend and catch truncation or retries that inflate it. The benchmark code will be published after open-source review.

In summary, the most cost-effective way to run an agent is to match each task to the model that fits it. Start with a fast model on a light setting. You'll often find it already gets the job done. Then add a small, almost free classifier like Jev to send only the hard tasks to a stronger model, and ask simple yes-or-no questions rather than one broad one. Judge every option by what it costs per task done right, not per token.

There is the argument that if you put it on the internet (not behind a paywall/login/etc.) then you are granting an implied license to anyone read it (you set world:read permissions on the file on your web server), but it is reasonable to require Google to pay for commercial use.

The old argument still holds that Google are exchanging value in-kind with sites by providing search indexing and directing traffic to the site, but if the AI summary uses the content, Google are providing less value than they used to do. They should pay for this difference.

InfoQ Homepage News Java News Roundup: TornadoVM 8.0, JNoSQL, Endive, JReleaser, JBang, LangChain4j, Quarkus Almanac

Nov 02, 2026 4 min read

This week's Java roundup for October 26th, 2026, features news highlighting: the GA release of TornadoVM 8.0; point releases of JNoSQL, Endive, JBang, JReleaser and LangChain4j; a maintenance release of JobRunr; beta releases of Hibernate ORM and Hibernate Reactive; and introducing the Quarkus Almanac.

Build 22 of the JDK 28 early-access builds was made available this past week featuring updates from Build 21 that include fixes for various issues. More details on this release may be found in the release notes.

For JDK 28, developers are encouraged to report bugs via the Java Bug Database.

The release of TornadoVM 8.0.0 delivers their new pure-Java compiler, known as the TornadoVM Compiler (TC), that compiles Java kernels to CUDA C++, OpenCL C or Metal Shading Language. This replaces the Graal Compiler and Java-Level JVM Compiler Interface (JVMCI) that have been integral to TornadoVM for the past ten years. Developers won’t need to change their code with TC. Classes such as TaskGraph, TornadoExecutionPlan and KernelContext, and annotations such as @Parallel and @Reduce will work with TC.

TornadoVM now takes advantage of GraalVM Native Image and JEP 516, Ahead-of-Time Object Caching with Any GC, under the auspices of Project Leyden for improved startup time. Further details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The release of Eclipse JNoSQL 1.2.0, a compatible implementation of the Jakarta NoSQL and Jakarta Data specifications, ships with notable changes such as: a new VectorTemplate interface to support vector databases; integrations of the Milvus, Qdrant, and Weaviate vector databases for vector search; and new fluent temporal query operations for working with time-series data. More details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The release of Endive 1.2.0 provides bug fixes, dependency upgrades and new features such as: support for SIMD instructions, by default, in the interpreter on JDK 11+ without the need to add or configure; improvements to Redline, their experimental, ahead-of-time (AOT) compiler, for more GC-friendly native memory; and continuous improvement on the Endive Component Model, an implementation of the WebAssembly Component Model Design and Specification, to support the Endive runtime. Further details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The release of JBang 0.143.0 delivers bug fixes, documentation improvements and two new features: replace use of SDKMan with mise-en-place as the former does not support WindowsOS; and a new unified syntax to execute a specific version of an alias. More details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The release of JReleaser 1.27.0 ships with bug fixes, documentation improvements, dependency upgrades and new features such as: the ability for the support for the JReleaser Tool Provider to be invoked and/or loaded using Java Modules; improved generation of the changelog when GitHub issues are linked inside commit messages; and the removal of XPath to parse POM files as there were issues when packaging JReleaser as a native image. Further details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The formal release (along with the thirty-third beta release) of LangChain4j 1.23.0 provides bug fixes, documentation improvements, dependency upgrades and new features such as: a new OllamaDecisionModel class to support decision models running in Ollama; and a new ConfidenceCascadePlanner class as a confidence cascade decision model-based agentic pattern. More details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The fourth beta release of Hibernate ORM 8.0.0 delivers bug fixes and notable changes such as: the ability to directly declare the @Filter and @SQLRestriction annotations on associations with the Jakarta Persistence @ManyToOne and @OneToOne annotations that include associations mapped using join tables; and a new @GeneratorOptimizer annotation that selects a "standard optimizer for sequence or table identifier generation directly on an identifier field or getter."

The first and third beta releases of Hibernate Reactive 5.1.0 and 5.0.0, respectively, ship with bug fixes and notable changes such as: a new platform Bill of Materials that manages the versions of Hibernate Reactive, Hibernate ORM and Vert.x SQL clients; and a version check during bootstrap to ensure that Hibernate ORM and Vert.x SQL clients on the classpath aren’t older than the minimum versions it supports. Both of these versions are compatible with Hibernate ORM 8.0.0.Beta4.

The release of JobRunr 9.0.3 provides bug fixes, documentation improvements and dependency upgrades and one new feature that updates the JobHandler class that improves the log messaging from the JobZooKeeper class. Further details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The first release candidate of Gradle 9.9.0 delivers notable changes such as: the ability for Kotlin DSL scripts to be incrementally compiled using the Kotlin Build Tools API for improved build performance; new test filters that may be applied to JUnit Platform tests that are not declared as methods; and improvements to the Problems API that provides a predefined hierarchy of problem groups from the Problems interface. More details on this release may be found in the release notes.

The Quarkus team has introduced the Quarkus Almanac, a utility for developers to compare changes between two releases. These changes include configuration properties, managed dependencies, extensions, and the API of the classes in the Quarkus jars. Developers can go as far back as November 2018 when Quarkus 0.0.1 was released.
