Presentation: Beyond the Harness: Canvas for AI Agents

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A monthly overview of things you need to know as an architect or aspiring architect.

QCon AI Boston (July 8-9, 2027): Agents. Context. Evals. Security. See how other teams make decisions. Register Now

Facilitating the Spread of Knowledge and Innovation in Professional Software Development

Unlock the full InfoQ experience by logging in! Stay updated with your favorite authors and topics, engage with content, and download exclusive resources.

Lily Mara explains how to avoid high-risk software rewrites through incremental FFI refactoring. She shares how engineering teams can replace Python bottlenecks with Rust via PyO3, demonstrating how to achieve dramatic function-level speedups, seamless integration testing, and meaningful infrastructure cost savings without microservice overhead.

The article argues that reliable agentic AI architecture depends on a context engine that selects, injects, compresses, and expires information before it reaches the model. It separates context, memory, and knowledge, and recommends tiered memory, curated knowledge, and token budgeting. It also covers common anti-patterns, such as context stuffing and stale data loops, with mitigations.

Nimisha Asthagiri shares how applying proven product engineering and architecture principles creates a bounded canvas for AI agents. She explains how this model provides agents with bounded autonomy, continuous drift detection, and decision records to scale software development resiliently.

Alasdair Allan discusses empirical research from METR and Anthropic on AI coding adoption. He explains the "supervision paradox," where reliance on AI tools disrupts junior skill development, risks institutional context loss, and threatens the long-term engineering pipeline needed to oversee autonomous systems effectively.

Colin Douch shares battle-tested strategies for building and scaling self-hosted observability stacks. He explains how to effectively correlate metrics, structured logs, and distributed traces using open-source tooling like Prometheus, VictoriaMetrics, and Jaeger, while navigating the engineering trade-offs of avoiding SaaS vendor lock-in.

Work through your production AI decisions with senior engineers from other companies. Apply their perspectives to challenges from your own work.Online. Register Now.

Building RAG pipelines or AI agents? Work through retrieval, context, evals, and reliability decisions with experienced practitioners.Online. Register Now.

Compare team design, delivery flow, AI roles, and metrics with experienced practitioners. Online. Register Now.

Distributed systems, decentralized decisions, platform engineering, and AI architecture. Online. Register Now.

What's working across AI, architecture, and leadership, from the teams doing it. Register. Early bird ends Nov 10.

Production AI across agents, context, evals, security, and infrastructure, from the senior engineers building it.Register. Early bird ends Nov 10.

What early-adopter teams have proven in production, across 15 engineering tracks. Register. Early bird ends Nov 10.

Learn from the decisions and trade-offs behind AI systems already running in production.Registration open.

InfoQ Homepage Presentations Beyond the Harness: Canvas for AI Agents

Nimisha Asthagiri shares how applying proven product engineering and architecture principles creates a bounded canvas for AI agents. She explains how this model provides agents with bounded autonomy, continuous drift detection, and decision records to scale software development resiliently.

Nimisha Asthagiri is a Technical Advisor at Thoughtworks where she leads client digital transformations using design thinking, change management, and platform architecture. Her current focus is architecting agentic enterprises with systems thinking for responsible AI.

QCon AI is a practitioner-led event focused entirely on the engineering discipline required to scale these workloads safely. It provides direct access to the architectural playbooks and failure metrics that peer organizations use in production.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

Nimisha Asthagiri: Today, I'm going to talk about, "Beyond the Harness: Canvas for AI Agents." I want to start with a story. I don't know about you, but for me, it feels like deja vu. Why it is, is because many moons ago, I used to be heading up architecture for an open-source community. In that community, we had a codebase that had grown organically. We had to worry about decay of the codebase. There were feature toggles that were not being garbage collected. There was code that people didn't know whether or not who owns it, what is the purpose of it, things like that. There was also drift. We had a modular monolith that we were going towards, but it was becoming a big ball of mud. Actually, it was, and we're trying to disentangle it. There was architecture drift that we were facing against. Then all of these things, we needed to have, therefore, a way to support the growth of that codebase and our community.

In order to do that, we needed guardrails. We needed context to everybody. We needed to be able to provide guidance and a digital strategy that everyone was rowing towards. For me, this feels like deja vu. We were also talking about, at that time, it was leading humans. It's open-source community, so there was teams across the world distributed, not necessarily, some in Australia, some in Boston. We didn't talk to each other synchronously. We needed ways to be able to communicate asynchronously, leave traces around, leave decision records around. While this concept of cognitive debt is at the rise right now with AI agents and work concerns, in 2021 and so forth, these were things that I needed to deal with even earlier. Why that is, is because we had humans that couldn't connect with each other. We had people who would leave the open-source community or leave the organization maintaining it. These were things that we had to deal with. We had humans who wanted the autonomy to innovate. I'm going to talk about that today, and how that might actually apply some of these architectural thinkings and principles to then us providing a bounded autonomy for AI agents.

I'm Nimisha Asthagiri. I work at Thoughtworks. I'm currently a technical advisor. I had my upbringings in software engineering and algorithmic work and so forth. Eventually leading to being chief architect at edX, which was a startup based in the Boston area before I joined Thoughtworks. I do want to caveat with saying we're all learning. The industry is changing rapidly. Some of the things that I'll talk about today please take that with a grain of salt because now applying it to a new paradigm shift that we're all going towards is something that we're all experiencing. However, some best practices, and this is why it feels deja vu, might be applicable here. That's why I want to share with you today.

Here are some main takeaways. Hopefully this resonates with you as a conversation to have together right now, so really some of the mental model shifts. What I'm going to do is I'm going to talk through some of this paradigm we're talking about, which is harness engineering. I do want to also put forth a proposal of maybe shifting our mental model from not just a controlled harness and what that might lead us to when we're talking about managing agentic topologies, but to thinking about a bounded canvas. We'll talk more about that. In addition to that, I mentioned about cognitive debt, and that is a huge concern. What might be our mental model if we don't necessarily question it but more so accept it and think about what are those traces, what are those decision records, not just architecture decision records, organization decision records, product decision records that are needed to leave as stigmergy or traces or pheromones essentially for whoever inherits the codebase and works on it.

We'll talk about some of these mental model shifts. I also want to be able to propose thinking a little bit more holistically, not just a particular coding task at hand, but let's leverage some of the things that we've been doing with operating models for product engineering overall and see how that could be applicable here, and think about this as a much more longer life cycle. Designing for change. We're talking about evolutionary architecture, but what might be some specific ideas and main takeaways I want to be able to share with you today as well as how might we be deliberate about actually deliberately disturbing our codebases and our products. We've talked about chaos engineering over the years, but I think it's so applicable right now, especially if we're not the ones actually writing the code directly. That's the takeaways.

How might we do this? Let's go through these three acts together. We'll start with the what, the why, and then the how as we talk through this. Let's start with the what. I'm talking about this as a semantic diffusion because, as you know, there's concepts that are coming forth so quickly, like prompt engineering was 2023, 2024. 2025 we have context engineering, and now in 2026 we're hearing about harness engineering. Really when prompt engineering, the need of the day at that time was to just be able to optimize that instruction, optimize that one communication that you're having with the agent. That's what we were talking about and how do we be more efficient with our tokens and so forth. Then context engineering, now you're talking about across interactions, and now a context where you're able to provide to these agents. What are they able to see?

Once again, token optimization for one thing, but also the quality of the output, those types of things come into play. There you're then managing the information exchange between yourself and the agents. Then finally you have harness engineering and this has been a big buzz starting February and so forth, and we'll talk through that. Now we're talking about no longer one task across sessions. We're talking about actually for an entire lifetime. Not just one interaction, but across a lifetime of what you might be developing as a product and so forth. In order to do that, you want to build a world. You want to build a system. Now systems engineering and those perspectives come into play.

Let's start with February 5th, earlier this year. We had Mitchell Hashimoto from HashiCorp who had published this in one of his blogs. I don't know if there's a broad industry term. Remember we're all making up terms as we're trying to figure some of these things out. For him he was like, this feels like harness engineering. What is he talking about? He's talking about whenever I find a mistake and I need to then solution for that mistake, then I'm going to update the harness so that that never happens again. This harness methodology, we were talking about harnesses, so like Cursor, for instance, as a technology which a human through the harness communicates to the coding agent. There's a lot of capabilities that are forming there. This harness engineering is now as a user you're updating that harness. That's what Mitchell is talking about here. You'll see that this is something that we're talking about now having a user updating of that harness as we're going forth.

We took that, and from that OpenAI started doing additional experimentation. Within five months they had a team at OpenAI build a million lines of code, and they were experimenting with this. The way that Ryan coined this was, humans steer and agents execute. Once again, this concept of harness. When Mitchell mentioned it a week before, he, I think, may have also been thinking about test harnesses. Our best practices are like, when something breaks, make sure that there's a regression test to make sure it doesn't happen again, hence his statement. Now we're starting to shift towards, there are these humans in control and humans steering so that agents can execute. This is interesting, but definitely a lot of learnings from OpenAI has worked with this, and we'll go through them in a bit.

Then now you have Kyle from HumanLayer, and he's also trying to make sense of this new terminology coming forth, harness engineering. He's thinking, how does that apply to context engineering? This was his mental model in March. He's thinking, maybe harness engineering is a subset of context engineering because context engineering is what you feed to the agents, and now with harnesses you're also establishing hooks and sub-agents and commands. He was thinking from a Venn diagram, is this how one might think about it? You can see how our mental model is evolving, February, March. Then another one with Vivek. Vivek from LangChain, he made it very simple. This has been quoted a lot now. He's like, really an agent is just essentially a model plus the harness. Anything that is not the model is the harness. When you think about a coding agent, you think about this very simple formula, and this is one way to think about this.

This now has been quoted, like I said, and cited in many different ways, and this is starting to then think about, ok, this is how we might think about it. It's essentially the white space that had been there. When we were having all these issues with unreliability with coding agents and the coding models, rather than waiting for the model developers to update their models, how might we as a consumer and a developer update the harness around it so that becomes more reliable? That's what this was talking about. You can see in Vivek's blog post, this is how he thinks about this. Overall, the entire thing is a coding agent and the harness, you have the model, but then you have things like the observability that we put, any persistence, across sessions, any controls, things like that. Those would all become part of the entire agent.

Everything in the white space in between, the agent and the model is a harness. That's Vivek's mental model in March. In that, he's also yes-ending, essentially, Hashimoto's statement earlier about this continuous upgrade, and whenever you discover something, whenever there's an improvement that's needed, there's this, therefore, a loop to continue to improve the models and continue to update the harness. This becomes now a training loop between model and harness and you as the developer, as a consumer of this. You can see this evolving. Then there was a publication. This is coming from a team in China, and they're starting to think also about, what is the overall system here? You have the model, yes, and then, ok, fine, the white space of the harness, which is then interacting with the environment over a period of time. Let's step back here and think of this now as an overall system.

Then now, if you think about this as an overall system, you're starting to now think about the behavior of the system and anything that now, as a software engineer, you want things to be auditable. You now want things to be able to be manageable and observable in that way. This was also a very interesting paper, I think, recommended. It's a good read. It has other concepts in there as well, and we'll come back to it in a bit.

Then Birgitta, who is a colleague of mine at Thoughtworks, she had a blog post in April on the Martin Fowler site, and her mental model, actually after some discussion amongst a few of us, she had published this and she was bringing forth this idea from cybernetics. We've been talking about feedback loops all the time, but there's feedforward and feedback. These are concepts from cybernetics, nothing new, but now applying it and putting a mental model for us. Maybe that's one way of thinking about it. Then the harness, therefore, encompasses all the guides and things that we might provide, the context, the best practices, decision records, whatever, as feedforward to the agent. Then feedback would be sensors, things that we might put in our CI pipelines or linting, and things like that. You can see that also being there. One thing that I found was there was something, going back to my architect days, I'm like, this sounds a lot like we're trying to control, and there's some maybe fear.

Of course, there is fear. We have to acknowledge it. Harness engineering definitely made sense even thinking about it as a right step in the evolution from prompt to context to harness. Actually, a colleague of ours, Mark, from Boston, he had this thing that he had put on LinkedIn, and I was like, yes, this actually resonates with me as well. He's saying, harness, guard rails, sandboxes, these are all when we're thinking about constraints from the outside, and externally we need to constrain these agents. He's like, the whole power of agents, the whole autonomy and self-learning, we want them to be able to self-learn, so there must be some things from within the system that also needs to be applied, not just externally. He was saying even our mental model, so I completely agreed with him, this mental model and mindset of harness is that limiting on how we might approach and look at this. He's also advocating for new metaphorical vocabulary.

Hence, I'm here with you today, and I'm proposing us thinking about it maybe as a canvas and not as a harness. I want to explain why. I found this poem, The Unfinished Canvas, by Mira Midha, and I found this to be actually very applicable here. The Unfinished Canvas, beautiful poem, and it talks about the artist's experience with painting and doing this on a canvas. It's a canvas that is only half filled, the other remains empty, and on one half of a canvas, but the other one has invisible words. This very much, I feel like as developers and whatnot, we have this creativity, and even when we're using and leveraging machine agents, there is this creativity where we're thinking about it's never done, but also there's incomplete, there's a backlog of items. There's a vision of where this product's going to go. It always might feel unfinished, and that's what we tell ourselves as well when we're developing products.

I think that is very applicable. Then the other one is, the mind did not play, the tune his hands wanted to. This is us, of course. We have these intentions. We want to have the intentional architecture, but then it's emergent design. We want to have these intentions on satisfying user requirements or improving our usability or whatnot, but users actually didn't use it in the way that we expected. There's a lot of that uncertainty, even in an artistic canvas as well as in our own work. Then brushes, clumsy, the bristles stiff as rigor mortis had set in. This is also the painter's experience where over time you keep using that paintbrush, and then there's some legacy there, and then there's a brittleness from the dried paint. This is our experience as well, where we see maybe some things that became overly constrained or overly defined over time, we kept adding to it, now became brittle, so our code becomes brittle, or we have the legacy, we have things like that that are lying around that we're unaware of, or really need to be able to address and detect that arises.

Then tin cans flung in disarray, anarchy from side to side. This is part of it, this is part of creation, is the messiness. In this poem, Mira's embracing it. I think we when we're developing and working with our teams, there is that messiness, and there is failures that come along to our successes. I think similarly, why cheat ourselves just because we're having machine agents part of our team now. Then, finally, view this mental storm from the out, to see not the unfinished canvas, not an end or a start, but a beautiful masterpiece that has become your art. Essentially, what this is saying is, step back, look at what you've created, and yes, it is unfinished, just like our products are unfinished, and over time, we collectively work on it, it becomes a piece of art. We've experienced this over the years, especially when we're working with our development teams, and not always, I know we have moments where we're so frustrated, it doesn't feel delightful, and our experiences aren't as delightful, but the potential of it being delightful is there.

Let's not cheat ourselves away from that, nor the machine agents who are helping us through this. That's the mental model I'm proposing with this canvas model. If we want to call it canvas engineering, I'm not here to actually coin the word as much as I am about guessing, being skeptical about the harness engineering term and its effect on what we might be, how we might approach it.

How might we then apply this? I don't want to overkill the metaphor here, but let's just go with it just a little bit more. Feedforward with this canvas way of thinking. You have a canvas. You have boundaries on the canvas. Artists, they might put tape around it to make sure you don't go beyond those boundaries, just like we might do that for our agents. You might scaffold it. Artists, before they actually start drawing and painting, they might sketch it to say, this is the architectural structure and modularity and things and subdomains and whatnot that we may want to have before we might jump in. Not always. Depends on the size of the project, of course. Many at times, if it's something that you've already prototyped and you know where you're going to go, that might be useful. They prep the environment. They put primers on the canvas.

For us anything that we may need to do to be able to establish any sort of scaffolding, or starter kits, or whatnot to help prime the environment, we would do that as well, even with machine agents. Then, any constraints. There's a palette of paint, colors that are already chosen beyond which you would not want to go to other color schemes. These are types of constraints that we may also have for when we're establishing any structural type of constraints for our agents as well. Then, finally, validations. What might be some things that we've already learned along the way, any prototyping and small experimental forms and things like that that then lead and become context to the paint and the canvas that we're about to put together. Then we have feedforward and feedback, a simple way of thinking about the system from a cybernetic standpoint. You have the structures.

Structurally there might be our type systems. We might have sandboxes for our agents, things like that. That still allowed bounded autonomy for the agents, but we're providing these types of boundaries. Reactive, we might have backpressure. We might have rate limits, things like that, and linters, and so forth that we also have our agents leverage. Then you have dynamism. You have some dynamic feedback guardrails as well. Those might be things that you want to be able to assess from a guardrail standpoint. Like, what are those drifts? What are those architecture drifts? What are those drifts from, the spec said this, but you're actually implementing this, things like that. If there's any budget runs, if there's any cost optimizations, things like that that we're going beyond. We have to be able to step back and see that. Finally, reflective, some reflective feedback. This is where you step back away from the canvas, look at it from the door and whatnot, and you're looking at strategically, where are we?

We dive deep and we're going in the rabbit hole and working through this. Wait, let's step back. Is this where we want it to be? Let's do any postmortems and retrospectives on how things are and so forth. This is all part of that experience, and hence the canvas experiences that we have. That was the what. That was talking about harness engineering, the evolution towards that, the verbiage, the taxonomy for that, and now also starting to think, what if we think about it as a system? What if we think about it as a canvas for creativity, but still with bounded autonomy?

Now let's go a little bit into the why. Why bother with this and why are we doing this? Some of this, I think we're all experiencing. The first thing, of course, is just the industrial pressures that exist in our own organizations. It's like, whether you want to or not, if you are in this industry, it is coming to us and has already entered our own goals and requirements coming from leadership. This is coming from McKinsey, of course, is one of the consulting firms, also consulting leaders in organizations on this whole AI movement. It starts with where we were, let's say, status quo. Then once GitHub Copilot, for instance, came in, and now you have developers always using AIs as an assistant or pairing with AI, maybe you might result in 1.2x if things are good, improvements in productivity. Then when you start having coding agents, so beyond just GitHub Copilot's assistant mechanism, but a coding agent with that harness and things like that, then maybe you might see 2x improvement.

Then, really, where people are looking to go, and this is where leadership are seeing this type of slide. They're saying, we can do even more because we can have a whole swarm of coding agents, and now everybody as developers, we elevate, and each of us become architects and so forth. That might be a 20x improvement. This is one of the whys. We've been learning as we've been experimenting with these coding agents. This one is coming from LangChain, Viv's blog post that I mentioned earlier. Here, what they found was that, if they take a model and all they do is change the harness and improve on the harness, they were able to have real improvements. They went from top 30 to top 5 on the Terminal-Bench 2.0's benchmarking. There is credibility around this way of thinking about leveraging harnesses.

I'm curious, have any of you experimented with harnesses? There are some starter kits and things like that on GitHub. This just came out in February. Still, this is something that we're experiencing. When we hear from people like LangChain, OpenAI, and these bigger companies, allows us to learn from them. This is definitely interesting, worth looking at their blog post on some of their learnings. OpenAI, as I mentioned, a million lines of code in five months. One of their lessons was just that we need to have continuous garbage collection, because now that we are going faster we can't wait to have that maybe a sprint zero or a sprint just for cleaning. Sometimes teams do that. We need to have continuous garbage collection. What they did was they launched separate agents where their role just was to do garbage collection. That garbage collection could be anything from code that's not used or detecting drift, feature toggles unused, anything like that as well.

This one, there's quite a few industry learnings here about not constraining agents. If we constrain the agents too much, then you get context bloat because you're saying all of these things, and then there's cognitive misunderstanding and communication interaction failures with the agent because it doesn't know what to follow. Those issues arise. Then, also, what we might be creating as agent skills, they might become brittle because there's too much complexity. We're saying do this and do that and do that. It's such micro-optimizations that any little change, the agent is not able to self-heal and recover because it's been told too many things. I think those are some learnings for us to consider.

In the article that I mentioned earlier by the team in China, they had a whole taxonomy of failures from their experiences. They talk about, for instance, it's possible that the context itself, the agent doesn't have the context that it needs. Maybe the tool that it's trying to fetch doesn't have access to it. There's any access issues to the tool, or it doesn't know which tool to discover, things like that. Maybe the feedback mechanism that we may have put into play becomes unavailable or is not accessible for any reason, or the agent doesn't understand it. The models themselves might have some failures that were unexpected. Then on the bottom row we have things that agents may not be able to verify for whatever reason. Entropy is going to continue to be there. That's just part of law of thermodynamics. Regardless of what we do, there's always going to be entropy.

When it comes to architecture, we're always managing entropy. That's going to be part of whatever we build. Even today without machine agents or in the past without machine agents, these were things that we would run into. Recovery, so maybe there is an issue in production or issue with some tests and whatever, and agents not able to recover from that. These are different types of agentic issues. Sometimes we don't really know the root cause. I do want to say while, for instance, models will continue to improve and the harnesses provided by our coding agent providers, they might focus more on the top and be able to bridge the gap between what they provide and what we're able to do today. However, the things on the bottom, like they're always going to be there. They always have been there even without machine agents. Just want to share that these types of failures, we're not here to control and prevent.

Instead, the alternative model is, how do we make ourselves resilient? Like the DevOps model, if something's going to fail and has failed, try to make it happen more often so that you have a much more resilient system so you know how to recover from those failures. Then, finally, another industry learning also comes from Birgitta, and she was sharing that a lot of the work today up to now has been more on the coding tasks related stuff and maintainability of code and things like that. What's a gap right now is behavioral stuff. Like, what about the product standpoint? What are those behavioral, functional requirements? How do we work with our coding agents to support those?

That's what I'm going to talk about actually in this next section, because we've been doing this. We have been working on functional requirements. Right now, our focus is on all the non-functional requirements. How do we now apply functional requirements to the coding agent world? Where I would like to start, this is actually coming from 2020, this is coming from my architecture experience earlier. I looked back at this, remember I said it feels very deja vu-ish, to like say, this is the product engineering operating model that we used at edX. Can this now be applied here where we're thinking about agents? I'm working with some life science clients right now, and we're trying to put together an operating model as we're working with agentic code and stuff like that. I looked back at this to see how might we do this. Then, whatever canvas we might develop for the agents, how might we leverage from here?

Let's take this one by one of what we're talking about. You'll see overall, even then, we were thinking about, and this is just the human world, the define phase, the build phase, and review phase. As a product organization, when we're defining, we're thinking about what's the overall strategy, product strategy, what are the opportunities, what are those hypotheses that we might want to test and experiment with. We might do things like Lean Value Trees. We might do some opportunity mapping and so forth. Then the output of that could be things like the opportunity trees, the strategy documents, and so forth. Then when we're building and we're iterating, continuous iteration, lean startup type of style, we might experiment as we do this, so continuous discovery as we're building. Then there, those outputs are specifications as well as any other compliance reports and things like that that we generate from our CI/CD pipelines and so forth.

That was always something that we also did. Then there's the review aspect. This part is interesting. I did want to call it out. I don't think the visual does it justice. It might be your customer satisfaction. It might also be your code quality, things like that. How does this apply to agentic coding? The organization here is essentially your agentic team topology, however you may choose to design that. Could be 100 agents working together. Announcing NetBSD 11.1

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Download 11.1 from our CDN: amd64 USB, amd64 DVD, i386 USB, i386 CD-ROM, sparc64 DVD, sparc64 CD-ROM, macppc CD-ROM, RISC-V devices, Cavium Octeon MIPS devices, Nintendo Wii, various Arm devices, full release dir with everything else.

Hashes, signed with the NetBSD Security Officer's PGP key, of all files in the distribution.

The NetBSD Project is pleased to announce NetBSD 11.1, the first point release of the NetBSD 11 stable branch.

An existing installation can be upgraded by booting an installation image and selecting the Upgrade option.

If you are using other update methods, update the kernel and modules first, then reboot and update your userspace.

This release primarily focuses on security and bug fixes, since our aim was to get it released as soon as possible to address certain security issues.

The complete list of changes can be found in the CHANGES and CHANGES-11.1 files in the top level directory of the NetBSD 11.1 release tree.

Complete source and binaries for NetBSD 11.1 are available for download at many sites around the world. You can download NetBSD 11.1 from our main CDN, or use a mirror site close to you. A list of hashes, signed by the NetBSD Security Officer's PGP key, is available for the NetBSD 11.1 distribution in this file.

NetBSD is free. All of the code is under non-restrictive licenses, and may be used without paying royalties to anyone. Free support services are available via our mailing lists and website. Commercial support is available from a variety of sources. More extensive information on NetBSD is available from our website:

The NetBSD 11.1 release provides supported binary distributions for the following systems:

Ports included in the release but not fully supported or functional:

The NetBSD Foundation would like to thank all those who have contributed code, hardware, documentation, funds, colocation for our servers, web pages and other documentation, release engineering, and other resources over the years. More information on the people who make NetBSD happen is available at:

NetBSD is a free, fast, secure, and highly portable Unix-like Open Source operating system. It is available for a wide range of platforms, from large-scale servers and powerful desktop systems to handheld and embedded devices. Its clean design and advanced features make it excellent for use in both production and research environments, and the source code is freely available under a business-friendly license. NetBSD is developed and supported by a large and vibrant international community. Many applications are readily available through pkgsrc, the NetBSD Packages Collection.

The NetBSD Foundation was chartered in 1995, with the task of overseeing core NetBSD project services, promoting the project within industry and the open source community, and holding intellectual property rights on much of the NetBSD code base. Day-to-day operations of the project are handled by volunteers.

As a non-profit organization with no commercial backing, the NetBSD Foundation depends on donations from its users, and we would like to ask you to consider making a donation to the NetBSD Foundation in support of continuing production of our fine operating system. Your generous donation would be particularly welcome to help with ongoing upgrades and maintenance, as well as with operating expenses for the NetBSD Foundation.

Donations can be done via PayPal to , or via Google Checkout and are fully tax-deductible in the US. See www.NetBSD.org/donations/ for more information, or contact directly.

InfoQ Homepage News LinkedIn Uses OpenTelemetry to Trace the Member Journey from Tap to Backend

Nov 03, 2026 3 min read

LinkedIn has built an OpenTelemetry-based telemetry platform that connects what members do in its iOS, Android, and web applications with the backend services processing those interactions.

The goal is to close a common observability gap. Client analytics can show that a member tapped a button or encountered an error, while backend tracing explains what happened inside the services handling the request. Historically, connecting these views required engineers to correlate separate systems. LinkedIn's approach allows a member interaction to be followed from the client through frontend and backend services to the underlying data stores.

A single page can generate numerous requests, each potentially passing through multiple services. LinkedIn introduced a pageForestId to provide context above individual distributed traces.

Each request retains its OpenTelemetry traceId, while related requests from the same page context can be associated through the pageForestId. Engineers can therefore investigate both an individual request and the wider activity that contributed to a member's experience.

This is important because conventional distributed tracing generally follows one request, whereas a member journey can involve many related requests.

LinkedIn operates a diverse technology stack spanning mobile and web clients alongside backend services written in Java, Python, Go, and Rust. OpenTelemetry provides a common model for instrumentation, trace context, and semantic conventions across those environments.

LinkedIn has built its own wrappers and extensions around the standard OpenTelemetry SDKs, allowing product teams to use consistent instrumentation while the platform handles concerns such as mobile lifecycle, export, and LinkedIn-specific metadata.

Client and backend applications generate OpenTelemetry spans, with trace context propagated as requests move through the system. Shared HTTP and gRPC frameworks automatically instrument common request paths, while teams can add explicit spans around important business operations.

Telemetry is transported through Apache Kafka and stored in ClickHouse, with pageForestId, traceId, and timestamps providing the keys for correlation. This allows engineers to begin with a member-facing interaction and drill into the backend execution responsible for it.

Capturing every span would generate an enormous amount of telemetry, making sampling fundamental to the architecture.

LinkedIn uses coordinated head sampling so related client and server activity receives a consistent sampling decision. A pseudonymised member identifier helps maintain that consistency across the journey, preventing the client from being sampled while the corresponding backend trace is discarded, or vice versa.

The company also distinguishes valuable quality-of-experience telemetry from lower-value debugging information. LinkedIn estimates that up to 90% of some backend tracing volume could potentially be treated as debug-level data.

Client-side observability introduces additional constraints. Mobile applications can be suspended or terminated before telemetry is transmitted, so LinkedIn batches spans but can flush telemetry when an application moves into the background.

The platform also applies controls around what data can enter traces, including attribute allowlists, denylists, and redaction. This is important because distributed traces can otherwise become an unintended channel for sensitive application or member information.

Traditional distributed tracing tools such as Jaeger and Grafana Tempo are primarily focused on following requests across backend services. Commercial platforms such as Datadog and New Relic also combine distributed tracing with real-user and application monitoring.

LinkedIn's approach is less about introducing a capability that these platforms cannot provide and more about making OpenTelemetry the common foundation connecting its client and backend telemetry. Its own pageForestId model adds a higher-level grouping around standard traces, while Kafka and ClickHouse provide a customised ingestion and storage architecture.

This also gives LinkedIn greater control over its observability stack and avoids making application instrumentation dependent on a particular commercial backend.

A member does not experience a traceId; they experience a page that loads slowly, an action that fails, or a feature that behaves unexpectedly. Connecting those experiences to the distributed systems behind them gives engineers a much more direct path from user impact to technical cause.

[$] The second part of the 7.4 merge window

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The 7.4 merge window, which closed on November 1, included a record-breaking 18,591 non-merge changesets. That is almost as many as the entire 7.3 development cycle, which had 18,765, itself a record breaker. Of those, 11,838 landed after the article covering the first part of the merge window. Most of those were bug fixes of various forms; it seems as though bug fixes may be crowding out feature additions across the open-source ecosystem to some extent. Still, a good number of new features were included, and a handful removed, in the tail end of the merge window.

Could a modern chess engine on a Pentium have defeated Kasparov in 1996?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

by Nicholas Carlini 2026-11-01

On February 10th, 1996, a computer program (IBM's Deep Blue) defeated the reigning chess champion (Garry Kasparov) in a game of chess for the first time. Deep Blue would go on to lose the match, but a year later, would get its revenge in a 3.5-2.5 rematch.

In order to achieve this feat, IBM spent several million dollars on research and development building a special-purpose computer specifically for this purpose. Deep Blue itself (the victorious, upgraded version) was powered by 30 P2SC processors each running at roughly 120 MHz, and (most importantly) 480 custom VLSI chess co-processors used to perform chess-specific operations like move generation and position evaluation. When it was built, Deep Blue was the 259th most powerful supercomputer in the world, and could analyze over 200 million chess positions per second.

But was Deep Blue necessary to beat Kasparov? Or could we have gotten away with an off-the-shelf 1996 desktop running a smarter chess engine?

As it turns out, we could easily have beaten Kasparov without building Deep Blue, using only commodity hardware, and it (almost certainly) wouldn't have even been close. I know this because, as I write this very sentence, I have sitting next to me a 200 MHz Intel Pentium Pro (released exactly 31 years ago---November 1st, 1995) with 64MB of RAM running a custom port of Stockfish that plays at an estimated Elo of 3300 ± 87 at classical time controls---far beyond the strength of any human, or indeed Kasparov's Elo of just under 2800. But wait, there's more! I've hooked this machine up to the internet with a Realtek RTL8139C Ethernet card, so you can play it below!

There are three ways to get stronger at chess: you can analyze more moves, analyze better moves, or analyze moves better. Famously, humans improve by doing the latter two: the average chess grandmaster during a classical game will analyze roughly the same number of moves as an amateur player, but they analyze only the most critical moves and do a better job with that analysis. Chess engines like Stockfish, running on silicon instead of our human brains, dominate us at the first of these and analyze millions of positions per second. And because there's not that much room for micro-optimizations on modern engines that will let a given CPU process more positions per second, most progress in chess AI also tends to follow how humans improve and finds better methods for selecting moves and analyzing boards.

Skill in chess is measured by an Elo score. A difference of 200 Elo means that you should expect the stronger player to win three out of every four games, and multiplicatively from there: a 400 Elo gap corresponds to a 90% expected score and a 1000 Elo gap gives a 99.7% expected score. The best chess engines that you could run on commodity 1996 hardware were rated roughly 2300 Elo. Deep Blue was rated 2700. In contrast, modern Stockfish is rated closer to 3700.

This raises the question: how much of the advantage of a modern chess engine comes down to the fact that a modern desktop computer is extremely fast, and how much of the advantage comes down to the fact that we've just gotten better at programming chess engines? As it turns out, the majority of the Elo gains (+720 of the +1335 gained) can be attributed to better programming, and not better hardware.

To test this, I ran two experiments. The first experiment uses Crafty, a competitive engine from 1996 whose source code is now available. On my Pentium Pro, the 1996 build of Crafty version 11.13 has an Elo of 1999 ± 90 at 1 second per move, and 2383 ± 77 at 10 seconds per move. On modern hardware (a Ryzen 7) this engine's Elo jumps massively to 2614 ± 97 at the same 1 second per move. But if I take this engine and slowly start to add all the knowledge we have about how to design better chess engines---adding modern search algorithms like fail-soft, LMR, correction history; adding better quiescence methods; and adding neural-net evals (NNUE)---then you can increase the Elo up to 2718 ± 34 on the Pentium Pro at 1 second per move, and 2974 ± 74 at 10 seconds per move.

In the second experiment I go somewhat further: instead of trying to update Crafty, let's just try to get Stockfish---the best chess engine today---running on 1996 hardware and see how it does. This takes some work and is mainly a matter of rewriting the code so that it uses 32-bit arithmetic (a PPro is a 32-bit machine) and doesn't use more than 64MB of RAM. This naive port has an Elo of roughly 2496 ± 64 at 1 second per move (2940 ± 76 at 10 seconds per move). But we can do better! A modern Stockfish engine is designed to run on a modern CPU. By making a number of adjustments to the algorithm and tuning it to the Pentium, we can increase its Elo to 2846 ± 35 at 1 second per move, 3109 ± 79 at 10 seconds per move, and 3180 ± 80 at 30 seconds per move.

The most concise way to summarize all of the above is just to have them play a match of 100 games against each other. And when I do this, I find that a modern chess engine running on a 1996 Pentium Pro wins 78, draws 17, and loses 5 games against the 1996 Crafty engine running on modern hardware.

The remainder of this article provides the details.

There's not much to say about the hardware, to be honest. I bought a used ("vintage" said the listing; I feel old.) desktop PC with standard period-appropriate hardware. The desktop is running a 200 MHz Intel Pentium Pro connected to an Intel VS440FX motherboard with 64MB of SIMM RAM clocked at 66 MHz. The operating system boot off of a 1.44MB 3.5 inch floppy disk, and connect to the Internet via a Realtek RTL8139C. I also have a 1MB Trio64V+ graphics card connected over VGA to my monitor, with input coming from a PS/2 keyboard---but I don't much use either of those and instead communicate with it over a serial port connected to my modern desktop.

For those readers who may not be able to appropriately contextualize this hardware, a 200 MHz Pentium Pro sold for $1,325 at launch in November 1995, and four 16 MB SIMMs would have cost about $2,000. Add on the other peripherals, and this machine would likely have gone for about four or five thousand dollars. So it's a nice workstation, but by no means a supercomputer. However, given the rate of depreciation and the exponential rate of improvements to hardware, this same machine in 1997, when the second match occurred, would likely go for just a thousand dollars (because who would want to buy a Pentium Pro when the better-in-almost-every-way Pentium MMX was now available for just $400?)

The interface between the chess engine and the internet is a custom-built "operating system" whose sole job is to spin up the chess engine and connect it directly to the networking card. (Throughout this article, when I say "I wrote", what I really mean is "I asked an LLM to write". I used a combination of Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 to implement the code here. (This article, though, is human text.).) It's designed to be as compact as possible: the operating system (which, in fact, is more like a hypervisor, or something) is sitting on a bootable floppy disk. Its sole job is to boot the Pentium Pro into 32-bit protected mode, initialize the networking card, and await further instructions. I can then send it any arbitrary "userspace" guest program that I want into RAM and it'll boot that up and start running it. The chess program I wrote starts up a custom-built HTTP server which waits for incoming HTTP requests with a given position (as a FEN string) and maximum run time, and on each request runs the engine until the timer expires, and returns a response.

As mentioned earlier I (again: a language model) actually modified two engines. The stronger of the two is powered by Stockfish adapted to run efficiently on a thirty-year-old processor. It plays at an estimated Elo of 3300 at classical time controls [a] Because I can't run nearly enough games at classical time controls on my single machine to get this number, I've extrapolated it from shorter time controls. I measured 3180 ± 80 at 30 seconds per move, and it gained +70 Elo per tripling of the time between 1 second to 10 seconds and 10 seconds to 30 seconds. Because a classical game gives about six minutes per move (including thinking on the opponent's turn) we should expect an Elo of 3350. Allowing for some diminishing returns, a safe estimate is an Elo of 3300. , high enough above Kasparov's 2800 Elo that we should expect Stockfish-1996 to come out ahead with a score of roughly 9/10. The (only slightly) weaker engine starts with the source code to Crafty 11.13, and is brought forward in time by adding all the lessons we've learned about how to write a better chess engine over the past thirty years.

The first engine I (read: my LLM) worked on was Crafty 11.13, its 1996 release. On this engine the LLM had to do the most work to backport ideas we've learned over the last 30 years. Below is a breakdown of where the Elo gains come from, when evaluating either at 1 second per move or 10 seconds per move. [b] Sometimes you'll find the 1s vs 10s improvements disagree. Part of this clearly could be noise---I only ran a few hundred games which leaves a relatively large margin of error---but there is also quite a lot of signal here. Because the 1996 computer is so much slower, at 1s/move it has a very low search depth and so how fast the evaluation runs matters more than how good it is. But at larger search depths, once you stop making tactical blunders, the position evaluation becomes much more important.

On the second engine, as mentioned above, I did the inverse. I (again, read: my LLM) ported Stockfish to run on 1996 hardware, and then tweaked the parameters so that it would be better suited to lower search depth. The gains here are thus somewhat more modest, because there are no large algorithm wins that remain.

I think it's overwhelmingly likely to be the case, but it's actually a little bit more tricky of a situation than you might hope. You see, I've done a bit of sleight of hand throughout this article. The problem is that Elo scores are relative, and I've been comparing my engine's Elo to that of various Stockfish engines that have known strengths on the Computer Chess Rating Lists (CCRL). But because humans don't play against engines very frequently, there can be some small divergences between scores on the FIDE Elo ladder and the CCRL ladder, in the same way a player's FIDE Elo doesn't directly translate to their Elo on online chess. Fortunately, because I've gotten these engines to play at an Elo rating of 3200-3300, and Kasparov was rated just under 2800, there should be more than sufficient buffer to absorb any discrepancy.

But if you happen to know a 2700+ Super GM who wants to try and defeat a 2026 chess engine playing on 1996 hardware, I'd be happy to accept the challenge!

The first time we humans do anything, we rarely do it efficiently. Once we see a path that would clearly work if only we scaled it up, we do just that. Only later, after we've had the time to better understand the problem at hand, do we come to recognize that we could have achieved the same feat much more efficiently by being clever.

I mostly did this for fun, because whether I could have beaten Kasparov with commodity hardware sounded like a fun question to answer, but I also hoped it would provide a tiny bit of insight into the rate of algorithmic progress in "AI". Chess engines in 1997 were already quite advanced; after all, Alan Turing helped write the first chess program nearly fifty years earlier. So I suppose at the time it seemed plausible that maybe there weren't that many more ideas left to find. And with hindsight it's easy to see that's wrong.

Which leads to the question: just how much are we leaving on the table with modern-day "AI"---that is to say, language models. Will the advances we accrue over the next few years from software outpace the advances in hardware scale? Clearly this toy experiment doesn't answer that question definitively, but it does gesture at a potential future. (There's also probably some kind of meta-lesson about using LLMs---"AI"---to speed up our ability to answer weird research questions like this.)

(Also, uh, if anyone has any ideas for projects I could do with a Pentium Pro in a few weeks once I no longer need it to host this chess engine ... do let me know!)

InfoQ Homepage News Vitest 5: Prioritises Performance with a Shared Vite Server, Trace View, and Stricter Test Defaults

Nov 03, 2026 2 min read

Vitest, the Vite-native testing framework maintained by VoidZero, has released Vitest 5.0, a major version that puts raw speed ahead of new surface area while quietly tightening several long-standing defaults.

Using a purpose-built benchmark suite that spans everything from a five-file utility package to a 1,280-module monolith, the team reports gains of roughly 8 to 25 percent across most setups, rising to 53 percent in VM pool scenarios. VoidZero summarised the release on X as "vm pools up to 53% faster, ~18% boost across the board including Browser Mode". The wins come from inline projects sharing a single Vite server, a stabilised on-disk fsModuleCache, fewer round trips between the main process and workers, and VM pools that reuse compiled code. A new vitest doctor command reruns a suite under alternative configurations and recommends faster options.

Browser Mode also gains a built-in Trace View that records each interaction and assertion as a DOM snapshot for step-by-step replay, while the new vi.when API lets a single spy return different values per argument.

clearMocks now defaults to true, unawaited async assertions fail rather than warn, hoisted vi.mock calls placed outside the top level now throw, and toThrow('') matches any error. As the independent OpenReplay breakdown puts it, most of these changes simply "break test code that was already wrong", but the dangerous ones are suites that pass locally on Vitest 4 and "go red in CI with an error that says nothing about why", usually call-count assertions that relied on mock history leaking between tests.

Vitest 5 requires Vite 6.4 and Node.js 22.12 or newer, and Yarn users must now install vite themselves because it became a peer dependency. The migration guide and v5.0.0 changelog document each change, and reporter output, attachments, and blob reports now live under one .vitest/ directory, so CI artifact paths need updating. Ecosystem catch-up is ongoing: Cloudflare's vitest-pool-workers issue notes its peer range pinned to v4 means any project pulling the new release "fails before a single test runs".

Against Jest, a recent head-to-head comparison scores Vitest ahead on performance and future-proofing yet behind on ecosystem and community, reflecting Jest's far larger install base and first-class React Native support. The advice to anyone considering the choice is unchanged, reach for Vitest on Vite, Nuxt, or greenfield projects, leave large working Jest suites in place, and let the two coexist in a monorepo.

Vitest is an open-source, Vite-powered test runner developed by VoidZero, the company founded by Vite creator Evan You that has joined Cloudflare. It reuses a project's existing Vite configuration to run unit, component, and browser tests with native ESM and TypeScript support, and is backed by more than 790 contributors.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

The Ubuntu 26.04 Rust sudo is not compatible with standard sudo

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're probably reading this page because you've attempted to access some part of my blog (Wandering Thoughts) or CSpace, the wiki thing it's part of. Unfortunately you're using a browser version that my anti-crawler precautions consider suspicious, most often because it's too old (most often this applies to versions of Chrome). Unfortunately, as of early 2025 there's a plague of high volume crawlers (apparently in part to gather data for LLM training) that use a variety of old browser user agents, especially Chrome user agents. To reduce the load on Wandering Thoughts I'm experimenting with (attempting to) block all of them, and you've run into this.

If this is in error and you're using a current version of your browser of choice, you can contact me at my current place at the university (you should be able to work out the email address from that). If possible, please let me know what browser you're using and so on, ideally with its exact User-Agent string.

I am not blocking Inoreader's feed fetcher or considering it to be too old, and it routinely fetches feeds from me. I don't know why Inoreader is showing you this page. It is possible that they're periodically trying to fetch feeds or pages with an old browser HTTP User-Agent (or an actual old browser) and taking the results of that fetch (this page) as what they should show people instead of the results of their syndication feed fetcher agents. This is a bad mistake today; the results of modern HTTP fetches depend partly on the HTTP User-Agent used.

Much like Inoreader, Feedly is periodically fetching my syndication feeds with a fake, old browser HTTP User-Agent header, which fails, and is then grimly latching on to the results for their actual feed fetching with their regular Feedly HTTP User-Agent. There is nothing I can do about this; you should contact Feedly support, if you can find them. See this comment of mine in Wandering Thoughts for more details.

Due to an ongoing attack, you may need to change the "User Agent Brand Masking" setting so that your Vivaldi identifies itself as Vivaldi, instead of Google Chrome. This applies to even the current version of Vivaldi.

You may be seeing this through archive.today, archive.ph, archive.is, and so on. Unfortunately, archive.* crawls pages to archive in a way that is impossible to distinguish from malicious actors. They use old Chrome User-Agent values, crawl from IP address blocks that are widely distributed and not clearly identified as theirs, and some of their IP addresses have falsified reverse DNS entries that claim they are googlebot IP addresses (which is something that is normally done only by quite bad actors). I suggest that you use archive.org, which is a better behaved archival crawler and can crawl my blog (Wandering Thoughts).

InfoQ Homepage News Google Launches LiteRT Tensor API for Building, Customizing, and Optimizing ML Models for On-Device

Nov 03, 2026 2 min read

Google has introduced the LiteRT Tensor API, a lightweight C++ API that allows developers to build, customize, and optimize machine learning models directly for on-device execution. According to Google, it combines the flexibility of native model authoring with cross-platform portability.

The traditional approach, Google says, is for developers to write computations manually in C++ for maximum control, which comes with a significant drawback:

Implementations are tightly coupled to specific backends (such as Metal, OpenCL, or Vulkan) and must be rewritten for each new hardware target. Furthermore, managing zero-copy buffer interoperability and synchronization across CPU, GPU, and NPU, while gluing together pre-processing, inference, and post-processing, demands substantial manual engineering effort.

The LiteRT Tensor API aims to address these challenges by allowing developers to build computation graphs operation by operation in C++. This provided greater control over model architecture, optimization, and accelerator execution, while enabling the same graphs to run through LiteRT on CPU, GPU, and NPU hardware without requiring a rewrite for each new target.

Beyond providing full control over model graphs, other key capabilities of the API include direct weight ingestion, allowing developers to load weights directly from checkpoints, including Hugging Face .safetensors files; zero-copy buffer interoperability, which avoids copying accelerator buffers across pre-processing, inference, and post-processing; and compatibility with AI coding agents, which can construct, modify, validate, and benchmark model graphs by generating C++ code.

Google used a coding agent to create a real-world app demonstrating how to use the LiteRT Tensor API for multi-object segmentation and tracking. The application also served as the basis for benchmarking performance across platforms:

We ran the same C++ code for the SAM 2 model on the GPUs of three devices: a macOS laptop (Metal), an iOS phone (Metal), and an Android phone (OpenCL). In our tests on the MacBook Pro, it ran about 1.5x faster than Apple MLX (fp16, image encoder plus mask decoder).

Google emphasized the fact that the coding agent could make a number of optimizations thanks to the model graph being defined in plain C++. Specifically, it "moved SAM 2's per-frame decisions, such as whether the object is still visible, from CPU code into the graph" and rewrote operations that triggered CPU fallback on WebGPU using GPU-compatible arithmetics.

Commenting on the announcement on LinkedIn, Arm director of AI Laurence Moroney emphasized that developers should always start with model conversion and turn to the C++ API only "when you need graph-level control or want pre and post-processing to stay on the accelerator". He also highlighted the benefits of defining model graphs in plain C++:

Going from under 10 to over 30 frames per second at 512x512 on WebGPU, with a coding agent doing the iteration, shows how much structured primitives help agents do real optimization work.

Google is also working on several planned improvmeents, including direct ML Drift backend support, additional NPU-friendly operations, automatic full-graph quantization, and expanded dynamic-shape for different image sizes, batch sizes, and sequence lengths.

LLMjacking Emerges as Thriving Cybercriminal Enterprise

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Cybercriminals have turned reselling cloud access to LLMs into a lucrative business model, with some victims paying as much as $1 million in resource consumption before access was revoked.

Cybercriminals have created a thriving business model out of stealing cloud access to commercial large language models (LLMs) and reselling it to paying customers, with the resource cost for victims reaching high as $1 million before access to their LLM was revoked.

The practice, called LLMjacking, has been around almost as long as LLMs themselves. However, now it's become an illicit enterprise on the underground market, providing a lucrative source of revenue for cybercriminals while victims are left to absorb the high cost of model usage, according to a report published Monday by Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) and Mandiant.

LLMjacking can create a massive spike in computing resources in a matter of hours, differentiating it from its more well-known cousin, cryptojacking, which gradually consumes resources. This has made it an attractive revenue stream for "a diverse set of financially motivated threat clusters operating out of multiple regions globally," Google researchers shared in the report.

Related:External Teams Chats Anchor 15-Minute Malware Delivery

"Across individual incidents, we observed fraudulent charges associated with resource consumption exceeding $100,000 USD in a single day, with cumulative charges at some organizations reaching $1 million USD before access was revoked," they wrote.

The report details how attackers gain initial access to LLMs and achieve persistence on targeted networks, and describes the service model around LLMjacking that has become a lucrative endeavor for cybercriminals.

To gain initial access to an organization's LLM, attackers typically identify publicly exposed LLM endpoints or obtain valid LLM application programming interface (API) keys, according to Google.

"The dominant initial access vector has been exposed or stolen cloud identity tokens — including identity and access management (IAM) credentials, service account keys, GitHub personal access tokens (PATs), and active session tokens — which threat actors leverage to establish an initial foothold into target organizations' infrastructure," the researchers wrote.

Some attackers also deliberately keep their initial activity quiet to gain access to an organization's LLM, according to Google. The researchers observed the use of minimal API requests, sometimes containing just one output token, to validate stolen credentials without generating significant consumption.

This sneaky way to gain and then sell access to the LLM could mean organizations are giving up valuable resources without even knowing, while the attacker is cashing in, notes Nick Tausek, lead security automation architect at agentic AI firm Swimlane.

Related:TiDB Breach Exposes Flawed Cloud Security Assumptions

"By the time someone asks why the cloud bill has exploded, the attacker may already have paying customers," he says. "Selling stolen model access gives criminals a reason to keep that access working for as long as possible, which makes the quiet activity before the spending spike especially important."

This notably represents a detection challenge for organizations that need to identify early indicators of compromised identities, unauthorized permissions, and suspicious access to AI services rather than just look for unusual consumption, he says.

The report outlines several examples of how attackers compromised and LLMjacked organizations, demonstrating that there is not just one route to take for successfully and illicitly gaining access to an LLM.

In the middle of the year, for example, a threat cluster tracked as UNC6993 compromised a cloud instance through a misconfigured service account key and used it to grant further access to an LLM API. Attackers also hosted a session hijacking page mimicking an application portal to join a group of LLM users.

"This page tricks users into navigating to the legitimate LLM service website, and pasting their raw JSON payload containing an active access token, which was then posted to the backend API to bypass password and multifactor authentication (MFA) controls," the researchers explained.

Related:Marvell, Microsoft & Oracle Work to Standardize Data Center Hardware Security

In another instance, a threat actor gained initial entry by combining a stolen personal access token (PAT) to access private code repositories and a suspected Web application vulnerability in a package running within a containerized cloud environment.

Similarly, a different threat actor used compromised cloud federation tokens to programmatically generate new access keys that allowed the attacker to bypass MFA protections, according to Google.

Once access to an LLM has been gained, it then needs to be sold for attackers to make money. The key to this part of the LLMjacking business model is what is called an LLM Mixer, which is an underground service that turns stolen access to commercial AI models into a subscription business, according to Google. These services aggregate compromised credentials, route customer requests through victim environments, and offer access to premium models at prices significantly below legitimate market rates.

As the researchers describe it, LLM mixers act as illicit middlemen, allowing cybercriminals to resell access to compromised enterprise LLM accounts without their customers needing their own legitimate API credentials or cloud infrastructure.

"In one investigation, GTIG observed two distinct operational clusters coordinating to compromise and monetize enterprise LLM infrastructure, demonstrating the sophisticated specialization emerging in this space," the researchers wrote.

Indeed, LLM Mixers are an important part of the emerging LLMjacking ecosystem because they provide the monetization layer that makes stealing enterprise AI access financially attractive.

Since LLMjacking relies on victims being unaware for as long as possible, the key to not getting dinged with a huge resource-consumption bill is to catch on as quickly as possible to the intrusion.

To do this, defenders should prioritize IAM and supply chain security, two of the most common pathways for gaining access to and abusing cloud services, according to Google.

Other best practices for preventing LLMjacking in a cloud environment include good credential hygiene by using a centralized secrets-management platform; least privilege by ensuring users and service accounts only possess the permissions necessary for their specific role and required tasks; and code monitoring by automating secret scanning within codebases such as GitHub and GitLab.

Routine configuration audits, rate limiting, network allow listing, budget alerts, and MFA are also good defense strategies, according to Google.

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.
