9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

Practical guidance on safer search, stronger login security, better executive protection, and other habits that help reduce enterprise risk.

9 Cybersecurity Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today’s Threats

Cybersecurity Awareness Month tends to bring no shortage of advice, but the most useful guidance for business readers is the kind that maps to the way attacks actually happen now: through everyday habits, trusted workflows, and small moments of inattention. Risks show up when employees trust a search result, follow a fake CAPTCHA prompt, scan a login QR code, click a social ad, reuse a weak recovery method, or assume that an AI model, executive device, or home network is secure by default. This article, compiled by the Dark Reading staff, and the accompanying videos bring those risks together in one place and focus on the practical behaviors and controls that can reduce them.

In October, a Dark Reading poll asked site visitors, "What's your biggest Cybersecurity Awareness Month challenge?" Readers identified phishing and social engineering as the top risk (39.1% of respondents), but that was neck-and-neck with shadow AI and risky data sharing (38.2%). A distant third was training fatigue and weak follow-through, identified as the top problem by 13.5% of respondents, and third-party and contractor access, with 9.2% of the vote.

Reader responses when asked, "What’s your biggest Cybersecurity Awareness Month challenge?" Source: Dark Reading

The following are nine tips that can move the dial on that enterprise risk in very real ways. A common thread for all of these is the fact that attackers win by abusing convenience, familiarity, and human trust, whether the issue is SEO poisoning, ClickFix, session hijacking, dark patterns, or dodgy ads on Facebook. That's why awareness training still matters, and why it shouldn't be a once-a-year affair but an everyday focus on a set of specific, repeatable habits backed by stronger enterprise safeguards.

For security leaders, that makes Cybersecurity Awareness Month a chance to do more than remind users to "be careful." It is an opportunity to push for better defaults: phishing-resistant MFA, tighter privilege controls, better reporting paths, safer browsing habits, stronger executive protection, and more realistic guidance for work that happens outside the office. The goal is to make the safe action the default action and to help employees recognize the red flags before a routine click becomes a business problem.

Embrace cybersecurity awareness in October and beyond with Dark Reading’s curated hub for security leaders — featuring expert news, analysis, and insights for hardening enterprise defenses in areas like identity security, AI risk, social engineering, supply chain, and more.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Becky Bracken, Media & Projects Director at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

As enterprises look to embrace agentic AI, they're sorting through frontier models, open-weight alternatives, and overseas offerings, and much of the focus is on evaluating the safeguards that, say, Claude might have vs. an open-weight alternative like Meta's Llama series. But for business leaders, the key question is not which model is inherently safe; it's whether your organization has put adequate controls around it. That means treating AI just like any other high-impact enterprise system that can be misconfigured, manipulated, abused, or compromised.

Every model type comes with tradeoffs. Frontier models may offer stronger vendor tooling, safety features, and governance support, but they can also introduce concentration and third-party risk. Open-weight models can give enterprises more transparency and control, but that control comes with more responsibility for hardening, patching, monitoring, and misuse prevention. Either way, the burden is on the enterprise.

For Cybersecurity Awareness Month, the practical takeaway is to assume compromise and build from there. Security teams should treat model weights, code, training data, prompts, plugins, APIs, and downstream applications as one attack surface. Limit what models can access. Restrict permissions. Segment AI workloads from critical systems. Log activity aggressively. Test for prompt injection, data leakage, and identity abuse. And keep humans in the loop for consequential actions, especially where money, customer data, or operational decisions are involved.

This is a golden opportunity for cybersecurity teams to become business enablers in the best sense of the term. AI will move fast, vendors will keep promising better safeguards, and the market will keep generating noise. But the durable security advantage will come from steady internal discipline. AI models can help the business, but they cannot be trusted to protect the business.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Alex Culafi, Senior News Writer at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

ClickFix is a simple but effective attack because it exploits something security teams deal with every day: people trying to solve a problem quickly. The user lands on a compromised or malicious website, a phishing page, or a fake browser-based meeting screen, and sees what looks like a normal verification or troubleshooting prompt.

But instead of asking them to check a box, the page tells them to open a system tool — often with Windows + R — and paste in a command that the site has already copied to their clipboard. Once they do, they can trigger the compromise themselves. Security researchers note that this technique has risen sharply and often relies on fake CAPTCHA or error messages that make the request seem routine.

No legitimate CAPTCHA, website, or meeting platform should ever ask a user to open Run, PowerShell, Terminal, or Command Prompt and paste in a script. A request like that should be treated as an immediate red flag. The attack works precisely because it looks like a harmless fix and can sidestep some browser-based protections by pushing the user to execute the command themselves.

Organizations should build this into awareness training now. Tell employees to stop immediately if a website asks them to copy-and-paste commands, press unusual key combinations, or "fix" an issue through a local system tool. Reinforce a simple rule: If a prompt moves from the browser into the operating system, assume it's suspicious until IT says otherwise. Also make it easy to report these incidents fast because early reporting can help security teams contain the damage and identify affected users quickly. Restricting access to system tools where appropriate can add another layer of defense.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Rob Wright, Senior News Director at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

For Cybersecurity Awareness Month, here's a useful reminder for business leaders and employees alike: Treat social media ads as untrusted content. According to Gen Digital research, nearly one in three (31%) ads on Instagram and Facebook lead to scams, phishing pages, or malware.

And according to Reuters, Meta is charging higher premiums for advertisers that are considered risky. So not only have these platforms turned into a virtual minefield of scams, but Meta has a financial incentive to keep these ads online.

That matters because malicious ads often don't look malicious. They may mimic legitimate brands, use convincing creative, and point to spoofed URLs that appear credible at first glance. Even security-aware users can get caught. The real danger is that these ads exploit trust, urgency, and familiarity — exactly the tactics that work on busy professionals who are clicking quickly on personal devices outside the protections of corporate security tools.

The best defensive habit is simple: Do not click directly from social ads to make purchases, log in, or share payment information. If an offer looks legitimate, navigate to the brand's site manually, use a bookmarked link, or search for the company independently. Before entering credentials or card details, verify the domain carefully. Organizations should reinforce this behavior in security awareness training, especially for executives and remote workers who often blend personal and professional device use.

Caution remains the first line of defense. Encourage employees to use payment cards with fraud alerts, enable multifactor authentication on shopping and social accounts, and maintain healthy skepticism toward "limited-time" deals delivered through ad feeds.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Arielle Waldman, Features Writer at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

Ransomware has moved beyond encrypting systems and stealing data. Increasingly, attackers are pressuring organizations by targeting the people around their leaders: spouses, children, relatives, and close friends.

The "Semperis 2026 Ransomware Risk Report" found that 37% of attacks involved threats against employees' friends or family members. In other words, if attackers can't break your defenses fast enough, they may try to break your people instead. AI is accelerating this shift by making reconnaissance faster and cheaper, and threat actors can mine social media, public records, leaked data, and digital breadcrumbs to map relationships, impersonate trusted contacts, and build highly convincing social engineering campaigns.

For Cybersecurity Awareness Month, organizations should expand executive protection beyond the corporate perimeter. Start with digital footprint reduction: Encourage executives and family members to limit public exposure, remove unnecessary personal details from social profiles, and use aliases or less identifiable display names where practical. Establish family verification procedures, including safe words or challenge-response phrases, but rotate them regularly because any phrase used in a compromised channel should be treated as burned.

Most importantly, extend basic cyber hygiene to the household: Require strong unique passwords, enforce multifactor authentication on personal and shared accounts, secure home networks and connected devices, and train family members to recognize urgency-based scams, impersonation attempts, and suspicious contact. A child's gaming console, a spouse's reused password, or a relative's overshared profile can become the opening attackers need.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Fahmida Rashid, Editor-in-Chief at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

Security awareness works better when it feels practical and relevant, not like a mandatory lecture. But one lesson is worth stating plainly: Some employees need more protection simply because they hold more power, more access, and more sensitive information. That starts with the C-suite, but it also applies to executive assistants, finance leaders, admins, and anyone else with privileged access.

Executives are high-value targets because they combine authority, visibility, and convenience-driven work habits. They approve payments, access sensitive strategy, and often log in from airports, hotels, and personal devices. That makes them especially attractive for phishing, impersonation, business email compromise, deepfake scams, and data theft. The right response is not to make life easier for them. It's to add smart friction in the places that matter most.

A strong executive protection program should include:

Phishing-resistant MFA, ideally hardware-based rather than SMS codes

Just-in-time privileged access instead of standing elevated permissions

Dedicated, locked-down devices for sensitive work

Stronger email and DLP controls to catch unusual forwarding, sharing, or exfiltration patterns

Focused coaching for executives and their inner circle, including assistants and chiefs of staff

Guidance for family and home networks, because executive risk doesn't stop at the office

Organizations can focus in on "white-glove" security, which means tighter controls, better support, and faster escalation when something looks off. If a control feels mildly annoying, that may be a sign it is doing its job.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Arielle Waldman, Features Writer at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

These design tricks train people to click quickly, surrender data reflexively, and ignore warning signs. Over time, that weakens judgment. Employees who get used to manipulative prompts may be less likely to notice when a request is truly malicious — whether that’s a fake consent banner, a spoofed login page, a bogus app permission screen, or a prompt asking for sensitive information. Dark patterns blur the line between annoying and dangerous.

For organizations, the goal should be to treat dark-pattern awareness as part of security awareness. Start by reminding employees that convenience defaults are rarely the safest settings. Public profiles, broad calendar visibility, third-party integrations, and data-sharing permissions are often enabled by default. Employees should be encouraged to review privacy and sharing settings manually rather than assume the platform chose the secure option. It’s also worth checking authenticator app settings for too-broad sharing privileges.

Another good rule: If you didn't sign up for a tool, don't create an account just because an email nudges you to. Unapproved signups can create shadow accounts that IT and security teams can't monitor or secure.

Finally, build a reporting culture around anything that feels off — surprise charges, mystery accounts, unusual permissions, or suspicious prompts. The more comfortable employees are raising small concerns, the easier it is to catch bigger issues before they spread.

Everyone knows password hygiene remains a challenge for most businesses, but risk in the login journey increasingly extends to the other seams around identity — the moments where users, devices, sessions, and recovery workflows intersect.

The threats include quishing, where a user scans a QR code from an email, webpage, or physical sign and lands on a convincing but malicious login screen. There's also push fatigue, where attackers bombard a user with approval requests until one gets accepted. And it includes session hijacking and token theft, where the attacker skips the password entirely and hops into an already authenticated session. Add SIM swapping to intercept SMS-based recovery, and the modern login chain starts to look brittle in several different places.

The long-term fix is to move toward phishing-resistant authentication, including FIDO2 security keys and passkeys, backed by enterprise controls that verify context, not just credentials. That means checking device posture, monitoring session behavior, requiring managed devices for sensitive systems, and reauthenticating when risk changes. In short: Don't just trust the login — trust the device, the session, and the user behavior behind it.

However, if this kind of full passkey rollout is not on the docket yet, there are practical steps organizations can take now to reduce login risk:

Turn on number matching for push-based MFA

Reduce or eliminate SMS-based authentication where possible

Shorten session lifetimes for high-risk apps

Require managed-device access for sensitive systems

Trigger reauthentication when device posture changes

And meanwhile, employee training should focus on a few clear habits: Don't scan login QR codes from unsolicited messages or unverified sources, for one, and never approve an unexpected SMS verification push.

To prevent session hijacking, log out of accounts when you finish using them, rather than just closing the browser tab. And avoid public Wi-Fi when you're doing sensitive things.

And finally, on the SIM-swapping front, switch from SMS MFA to Authenticator Apps that can't be intercepted via a phone network. You can also request that account changes require physical ID in-store or multilayered, out-of-band authentication before processing SIM changes.

Account takeover attacks are a bugbear that is unlikely to disappear anytime soon, but increasing friction and cost for attackers throughout the login process can really help and, hopefully, get you out of the low-hanging-fruit category at the very least.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Rob Wright, Senior News Director at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

Fall youth sports season means many parent-professionals are working from the sidelines — at soccer fields, hockey rinks, football stadiums, and school gyms — often on whatever free Wi-Fi happens to be available. That may not sound as risky as logging on at a major airport or hotel, but it still creates an opportunity for mistakes that attackers, pranksters, or opportunistic snoops can exploit.

Sophisticated nation-state actors are probably not camping out at your local rink. But there's still considerable risk if you connect to the wrong network. A hotspot with a name that closely resembles the venue's legitimate Wi-Fi can be enough to trick a busy parent checking email between periods or joining a meeting from the bleachers. Even a spoofed network set up as a joke can expose traffic, credentials, or session data if employees are careless.

Business readers that want to avoid their employer's penalty box should treat public connectivity anywhere as untrusted by default. Before joining a network, verify the exact Wi-Fi name with venue staff or posted signage. Disable auto-join so your device does not connect to similarly named networks without your knowledge. Use only approved secure remote access tools, keep endpoint protections enabled, and avoid handling sensitive work such as admin access, financial approvals, or password resets over public Wi-Fi unless it is absolutely necessary.

If you need connectivity, a personal hotspot is often the safer option — but only if it is configured properly. Use a strong password, change the default network name if it reveals your identity, and make sure the hotspot is not left open to nearby devices.

The broader lesson for Cybersecurity Awareness Month: Low-profile locations can still produce real security failures. The setting may feel routine, but the network risk is not.

Watch the video on this cybersecurity awareness tip from Alex Culafi, Senior News Writer at Dark Reading, on LinkedIn.

SEO poisoning turns a routine Google search into an attack path. Threat actors either build lookalike websites or compromise legitimate ones, then manipulate content and code so those pages rank highly in search results. The idea is that a user searching for software, a login page, or a partner site might simply click the top result, which happens to be poisoned, and subsequently hand over credentials or download malware.

Guidance on spotting fake websites specifically warns that SEO poisoning can push malicious sites to the top of search results, and Trend Micro’s 2026 research on more than 35,000 fake World Cup-related sites shows how easily attackers can industrialize fake-site campaigns around high-interest topics.

For business users, the safest habit is to stop trusting search rankings as a signal of legitimacy. Before clicking, inspect the URL for misspellings, extra words, odd country codes, or brand names that look slightly off. After clicking, be suspicious if the page immediately pushes a download, asks for credentials too early, triggers repeated redirects, throws pop-ups, or trips a browser warning. Those are strong signs that something is wrong.

Organizations should reinforce a few simple rules in awareness training:

Use bookmarks or manually typed URLs for key business apps, vendors, and partner portals.

Verify domains before downloading software or entering credentials.

Treat urgent install prompts and so-called ClickFix instructions as red flags.

Report suspicious search results, spoofed pages, and accidental downloads to IT immediately.

Isolate the device and change credentials quickly if a user may have interacted with a fake site.

The practical message for Cybersecurity Awareness Month: Search is a convenience layer, not a trust layer. Employees don't need to become threat hunters, but they do need to slow down long enough to verify where a search result is actually taking them. That pause can prevent a compromise.

Germany Busts 'Crime-as-a-Service' Investment Fraud Outfit

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

German police led a multinational probe that exposed an alleged crime-as-a-service provider supplying fraudulent trading platforms to investment scammers.

You see an online advertisement promising quick returns from trading cryptocurrency, foreign currencies, and stocks. You give the "investment firm" your information, and an operator calls you and convinces you to transfer a small amount of money into a platform. You make early profits quick, so you deposit increasingly large sums of money. But when you attempt to withdraw your earnings, the operator stalls for a while until finally cutting off contact.

This pattern is familiar to victims of investment fraud, a multinational criminal industry that relies on social engineering to convince would-be-investors that they're about to make substantial gains before pulling the rug out from under them.

Europol today shared news of the disruption of one such operation, an investment fraud scheme with potential victims across more than 50 countries and a company allegedly supplying fraudulent trading platforms and leads to criminal groups. According to a press release, Europol and Eurojust supported an international operation involving authorities in Albania, Cyprus, Finland, Germany, Israel, Kosovo, and North Macedonia.

Related:Investors Dish on New Startups & What They'd Back Next

The German Itzehoe Public Prosecutor's Office led the operation, targeting a company suspected of supplying phony trading websites and multiple call centers to criminal groups; investigators estimate losses to exceed 4 million euros.

International authorities searched 17 locations, including a call center in Kosovo, leading to the seizure of 100 servers and the arrests of seven suspects.

The law enforcement action reflects a multiyear investigation that began in 2022, according to Europol, when a German couple reported they could not withdraw money invested through an online trading platform. This led to two previous "action days" by authorities, one in 2022 (involving two arrests and the seizure of evidence) and another in 2025 (involving more arrests and seizures of money and evidence).

"Analysis of the material recovered exposed the suspected infrastructure provider and its links to call centre operators, paving the way for the latest operation," the press release read.

A Eurojust press release provided further detail regarding the extent of the operation. The analysis of evidence seized during the second action day exposed a company based in Cyprus and Israel allegedly offering "crime-as-a-service," supplying criminal groups with fraudulent websites that funnel targets into the central investment scam.

The data of potential victims visiting these websites and expressing interest would be sold as leads to call centers, which contacted the victims to persuade them to invest their money, leading to repeated demands for additional investments. Authorities identified suspected call center-operating clients of this company in Albania, Kosovo, and North Macedonia.

Related:Behind the Scenes of How Operation Blackout Went Down

The fraud operation guaranteed singificant returns for trades in cryptocurrencies, foreign currencies, or stocks, according to Europol. But authorities found that the seemingly legitimate platforms were run by "brokers" that used phony profits and social engineering tactics to convince victims to keep investing. Additionally, investigators also found the operators used affiliate marketing services and deceptive advertisements to lure in potential victims.

Austin Anderson, director of threat intel at cybercrime disruption firm Netcraft, tells Dark Reading that operations like these that target upstream providers can help disrupt investment fraud operations at scale.

"Fraudsters have increasingly specialized in one piece of an attack's supply chain, as seen in this scheme where websites collected personal data that was sold to call centers to ultimately coerce funds from victims," he says. "While this can breed sophistication in each actor's niche, it also introduces fragility when a large dependency like the call center is disrupted. Ultimately, without arrests that remove fraudsters from the market, these operations only temporarily disrupt operations while actors rebuild their infrastructure."

Fresh European Fridge Attacks Put Cold-Chain Security in Focus

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Beyond food storage, the same combination of Internet connectivity, remote access, and inadequate security practices can put temperature-controlled infrastructure across other critical sectors at risk.

A second wave of attacks on supermarket refrigeration systems across Europe this week has heightened concerns about the broader "cold chain's" vulnerability to similar attacks, given that supermarkets aren't the only businesses that rely on cold storage.

As in mid-October, an unknown threat actor appears to have gained access to refrigeration controllers at multiple European supermarkets and switched the systems to defrost mode, raising temperatures to 50 degrees Celsius (122 degrees Fahrenheit) and causing food spoilage.

Reporting by the BBC, Reuters, CNBC, and other outlets suggest the latest attacks were smaller in scope compared to the first wave in October that impacted more than 700 retail and grocery stores primarily in France. A smaller number of stores in Belgium, Germany, the Netherlands, Portugal, Poland and Spain were also caught up in the October attacks.

Related:Les supermarchés de l'UE paient d'un firmware de réfrigérateur non mis à jour

BBC reported the new attacks as impacting "dozens of supermarkets" in seven European countries including Belgium, Luxembourg, Estonia, Ireland, Sweden and Finland. Spokespersons from several supermarket chains like France's Carrefour, Belgium's Ahold Delhaize and Colruyt Group, and Sweden's Coop chains confirmed varying but relatively limited impacts from the new attacks.

In a Nov. 2 statement, Carrefour said a cyberattack targeting its refrigeration systems had impacted 15 out of 700 stores in Belgium. In an undated statement, US-based Copeland, which manufactures technologies for regulating commercial refrigeration systems, alluded to a cybersecurity incident affecting refrigeration systems in France, which it said related to a discontinued generation of supervisory controllers.

No individual or group has claimed responsibility for either the first or the second wave of attacks, and the motivation behind it remains a mystery. European authorities are currently investigating the attacks.

Researchers, including those at France's CERT, have linked the hacks so far to Internet-connected refrigeration controllers made by Denmark-based Danfoss, Italy-based Dixell, and Copeland. Organizations using these controllers often connect them to the Internet to enable remote maintenance and support, but in doing so also potentially expose them to unauthorized access and remote attack by threat actors.

Risks include unpatched vulnerabilities in these technologies that attackers can exploit, like three in Danfoss' AK-SM 800A controllers that researchers at Claroty disclosed this August and another two they reported the same month in Copeland controllers. Weak configurations and poor security hygiene are also other major problems.

Related:AI Takes Control of Robotic Arm in 16 Minutes Flat

"Misconfigurations and systemic hygiene failures represent the vast majority of the threat surface," says John Gallagher, vice president at operational technology (OT) and Internet of Things (IoT) security vendor Viakoo. "While unpatched vulnerabilities exist in older firmware, they are rendered dangerous only because basic architectural hygiene was skipped, especially [when] making them Internet-facing."

Common mistakes include widespread use of default passwords, vendor-shared service codes, or static credentials across hundreds of locations makes brute-forcing trivial, Gallagher adds.

The weaknesses are not confined to supermarket refrigeration systems. The same combination of Internet connectivity, remote access, and inadequate security practices can put temperature-controlled infrastructure across other critical sectors at risk, according to security experts.

Sean Tufts, field chief technology officer (CTO) at Claroty, points out that "pharmaceutical storage and distribution, food distribution centers, and industrial facilities all rely on connected operational technology to maintain precise environmental conditions."

Related:EU Grocers Pay a Steep Price for Unpatched Fridge Firmware

The level of exposure will vary depending on how these systems are deployed, connected, and secured. But many organizations that rely on remotely accessible refrigeration or temperature-control equipment face similar challenges around asset visibility, third-party access, and securing legacy systems, Tufts says. "The same types of weaknesses can exist across different industries, even if the equipment and operational requirements differ."

The potential consequences of a compromise, says Tufts, can vary by sector.

"A disruption to pharmaceutical storage could compromise temperature-sensitive medicines, while an incident affecting food distribution could lead to spoilage and supply chain disruptions," he notes. "Industrial environments may face additional operational consequences depending on the materials and processes involved."

The supermarket attacks are a reminder that refrigeration and temperature-control systems are part of essential operations and not just back-of-house equipment, Tufts adds. Organizations should identify these assets and know how they connect to the Internet and third parties.

"Where remote access is necessary, use a VPN, strong authentication, or a centralized secure-access tool with session tracking." Changing default passwords, keeping firmware current, and segmenting are also recommended security practices, he says.

Gallagher says that while it's unclear how the attacks on the commercial refrigeration systems played out, the most straightforward theory is the threat actor likely compiled a target list by scanning the public Internet for specific controller fingerprints, such as those tied to Danfoss, Dixell, and Copeland. They then likely deployed an automated script across that target list to exploit known web vulnerabilities or push default credentials, triggering the defrost mode simultaneously.

Gallagher says the same attacks that worked on commercial refrigeration systems can potentially work across almost any form of OT and IoT systems. That's because these systems often are managed outside of IT, lack automated controls to keep firmware and passwords updated, and have porous network borders. "The growth over the last three years in operational ransomware and in cyberattacks causing physical damage shows that these attacks are on the rise and not specific to any one kind of system."

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

Microsoft Patches More Than 1,400 Security Flaws

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Microsoft today issued updates to fix more than 1,400 security vulnerabilities in its Windows operating systems and other software. Today’s epic patch batch dwarfs September’s record of nearly 1,000 fixes, which was already about ten times the normal monthly patch load from Redmond.

Just one of the weaknesses patched this week is considered a “zero day,” or already exploited in the wild. CVE-2026-96951 is a security feature bypass in the Windows Shell that affects Windows 10 and 11 systems, as well as some Server versions.

Windows 10 users might be wondering if they can still expect security updates after this month. Microsoft had been slated to stop shipping updates for Windows 10 systems in October 2025, but ended up adding another year of free updates after considerable pushback. A few months ago, Microsoft quietly extended that support for another year (until October 2027).

At least 78 of the flaws fixed this month are rated “critical,” meaning they could be exploited with little or no action by the user, and most of those critical bugs allow an attacker to execute code of their choice on the vulnerable system.

While Microsoft said there are no signs anyone is exploiting it yet, a security feature bypass in the .NET Framework (CVE-2026-108398) was publicly disclosed prior to today, potentially giving attackers advance time to work out how to exploit the bug.

Satnam Narang at Tenable said the number of flaws fixed this month far surpasses the previous record-setting year 2020, when Microsoft patched 1,261 CVEs.

“We’ve now crossed the 4,000 CVE mark with two months left to go,” he said.

Microsoft credits large language models for the high volume of bug discovery, and presumably AI gets credit for many of the patches used to fix these flaw. Meanwhile, security experts have been warning that these unusually large patch volleys from Microsoft are placing extra strain on corporate security teams, which often have to test individual updates before deploying them across a network to ensure the fixes don’t cause problems for other software.

Tyler Reguly, associate director of security research and development at Fortra, notes that just 26 Microsoft vulnerabilities appeared in the “Known Exploited Vulnerabilities” (KEV) catalog maintained by the U.S. Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) so far this year, and security teams would do well to prioritize patching those 26 vulnerabilities first.

“Security leaders should focus on exploitable risk, and ask whether their teams have the clarity, capacity and support to act,” Reguly said.

Which vulnerabilities deserve the most scrutiny in your organization? The folks at Cisco Talos have a useful roundup of the more noteworthy flaws that Microsoft fixed today, and of course the SANS Internet Storm Center has a reliable roundup that indexes vulnerabilities by severity, CVE number and affected software.

One has to wonder how big the backlog of flaws is at this point. Maybe this month’s increase in the number of fixes just reflects an increasing number of people working on these fixes, and that they already have enough flaws to keep them all busy for a long time. Oh to be a fly on the wall.

Why do I still own and use a computer for my business?

For every invention, product, or process that man creates, several other problems are created for him. There never seems to be an end. I am glad that humans are actually testing the vulns that are discovered by LLMs, but for how long will that be the case? How long until the LLMs themselves well be checking there own vuln discoveries, testing and deploying with no human involvement…….

It comes down to basic Human Nature; biologically, we are still pretty much the same as our ancestors 0f 100,000 years ago. When the internet came along, what we did with it was pretty much what we did with nearly any new invention since then:

1. Get very excited about it 2. See if we could either: A. Stick it in our shorts B. See if it could be made a good weapon C. Amuse ourselves with it to the point of unconsciousness with it, or D. Enrich ourselves with it.

Unfortunately, ancient humans, when they picked up a rock, thought first about who they could bash over the head with it, not build a hospital.

I exaggerate a bit to make a point. We’ve come a little way since then, but that old hard-wiring is hard to erase. I imagine that, AI being made in our own image, will follow a similar course if not VERY strictly overseen.

Note: An old philosopher said that if we ever did produce something like an AI android, we would very shortly be very, very ashamed of ourselves.

I tend to disagree with the statement that the large number of CVE’s are placing strains on IT teams in terms of patch prioritization. That’s not the issue. For most vendors, Microsoft included, a single cumulative security update is issued, or a small handful for core products (Windows, Office, .Net). Whether those updates patch 100 or 10,000 individual CVE’s, the number of updates to test hasn’t really changed all that much. What HAS changed is the potential for the update to break things since more code is being modified with each update. Testing and deployment processes haven’t changed; it’s the monitoring for unexpected behavior during the testing phase that has become the pain point.

I agree. In my experience, for office workers, it’s low risk that patches will brake things. The factory floor is different. But even there, it’s not unusual to find PC’s running equipment not connected to the network due to the fact that they have to run proprietary software that won’t run on modern versions of Windows, or the PC-equipment interface is through a ISA or other legacy cards that modern versions of Windows do not support, and there is no way other than air-gapping to keep them secure. That’s why there are entire companies like NIXSYS (https://nixsys.com/) that build legacy computers using old CPU’s, old glue-chips & busses, and even old versions of Windows. No one junks a perfectly good but ancient CNC because it’s ancient PC died.

This was what I came here to say: it’s not the number of patches because everything gets rolled into one cumulative update. And Reguly is misinformed at best if he thinks IT teams can simply focus on the 26 most important updates – because we cannot select the 26 that are being exploited without also patching the CVEs that aren’t being exploited in the wild but for which the patch breaks, I don’t know, let’s say printing. Again.

So, what’s the patch record for Linux? search says patches published anywhere from 5~10 days after public notices are posted, not easy to match Microsoft’$ record…

Linux is starting to accept LLM vulnerability requests and they’re finding some flaws that have been around for decades. The total number is likely less, at least for the Linux kernel, but full distros use a large number of projects to create their OS and each of those pieces will have their own vulnerabilities.

See for example: https://security-tracker.debian.org/tracker/status/release/stable

That shows what’s currently being worked on for Debian. The first column notes the specific package the CVE refers to.

Should we call it a vulnerability if no attacker has any interest in it? I’m seeing a flood of vulns getting patched, but I’m not seeing a flood of vulns causing breaches…

If someone can make your mom stop, cry, and fork over a twenty to an animal shelter talking about puppies in a subway station, that is still a vulnerability of your mother, even if she is fortunate enough not to be approached on any one single given day of her daily commute.

I expect better analogies from you from here on in young man.

Philippine Migrant Worker Data Likely Leaked in 69GB Government Breach

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

An unknown operator appears to have exfiltrated SQL Server backups from the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration in under a half hour.

An unknown threat actor managed to gain access to sensitive databases belonging to the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration, exfiltrating nearly 69GB of information on workers, contracts, and financial records, according to an analysis of a staging server used in the breach.

Over 24 minutes, the attacker copied 27 backup databases with information on overseas-employment case management, including accreditation, contracts, and cashier and LandBank records, according to threat intelligence firm Hunt.io's Oct. 28 analysis of the logs found on a Singaporean staging server used by the attacker. The cyberattackers used privileged escalation tools, namely GodPotato and SeImpersonate, and the Impacket network library for Python to move the data, but the trail stopped at the staging server, says Esteban Borges, head of research for Hunt.io.

The server had been later accessed, but by who is not clear, Borges says. While Hunt.io has not yet discovered the data as advertised for sale, the information may have been detected and removed, but that seems unlikely, he says.

Related:State Bank of India Suffers a Customer PII Data Leak

"I won't claim the backups stopped on the staging server," he says, so it's "still a breach, since the data was copied out of the agency’s control and the log shows the transfer completing."

This is not the first time that cybersecurity firms have found evidence that Philippine government data had been stolen. In August, Hunt.io discovered another threat actor's workspace hosting a number of tools and, in that case, 1.2GB of data stolen from a Philippine nuclear regulatory agency and a maritime shipbuilding contractor. A year ago, Philippine police arrested the mayor of a small town after discovering she was a Chinese national posing as a Filipina citizen and operating a cyber-scam center.

Whether the current incident involved China or cybercriminal groups is not clear. While Hunt.io did find some Chinese language fragments in the parts of the code, that code belonged to a third-party software library; thus, Hunt.io did not attribute the hack. The company did say, however, that both cybercriminals and nation-state adversaries would find value in the personally identifiable information (PII).

"That kind of migrant-worker PII, plus contract and payment data, is useful for fraud and identity theft, so it has commercial value," Borges says. "It's also a detailed map of a diaspora population, which can interest a state actor."

The latest insight into attacks on the Philippine government came after Hunt.io researchers discovered an open directory on a server hosted on the Singaporean address space of a Helsinki-based firm UpCloud Ltd., the analysis stated. The open directory contained tools and logs files for the attack, including a rebranded version of the GodPotato privilege escalation library, a PowerShell stager to unpack tools in memory, and the full Impacket Python environment.

Related:Russian Influence Operation Widely Targets Latin America

While Hunt.io considered the incident a confirmed compromise, the lack of runtime telemetry meant that Borges could not answer where the attacker moved the data.

"[W]e still can’t answer ... how they got in, whether the payload ran, and where the data went," he says.

However, the tools and the attacker's process indicated an operator with some strong ability, but who also made some simple mistakes, Borges says.

"The payload engineering is competent, [but] the operational security is poor — they left the staging server and its logs wide open," he says. "[It was] someone who can build the tool properly but was careless about keeping their own server quiet. I wouldn't call that top-tier or amateur."

The impact of the breach may boil down to the identity of the attacker and what they do with the stolen data. While Hunt.io notified the CERT Philippines (CERT-PH) and the Department of Migrant Workers (DMW), the company declined to provide any more details of the national responses to the leak.

Related:Venezuelan Cartel's Malware Honcho Nabbed for ATM Jackpotting

However, Borges did say that, so far, the information has not appeared for sale on the Dark Web.

"We haven't seen the backups surface for sale or in a leak," he says. "We never opened the backups themselves."

Governments in the region need to scan their own networks and lock down their attack surface area, he says. Workers with too many privileges allow attackers with tools such as SeImpersonate to gain more access to assets on the network. Hunt.io also found that the migrant-worker portal exposed a number of risky ports, including SMB on 445 and WinRM on 5985 and 47001.

Rob is an award-winning, veteran technology journalist of more than 30 years, reporting on global cybersecurity issues, the latest offensive and defensive technologies, malware incidents, cyber conflict, and AI's impact on software and cybersecurity.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Recent reports include analyses of the shortage in cybersecurity workers, annual vulnerability trends, and annual threat reports.

Rob holds degrees from Cornell University in Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (double major).

U.K. Sanctions Cryptomus for RU Money Laundering

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The United Kingdom has issued economic sanctions against Cryptomus, a chameleonic cryptocurrency platform that experts say serves as a major conduit for payments related to ransomware, narcotics, darknet markets, fraud and cybercrime services that are helping Russia circumvent existing financial restrictions.

Heleket’s support agent on Telegram claims, despite all evidence to the contrary, that it is not affiliated with Cryptomus. Image: Richard Sanders.

On October 8, the U.K. issued sanctions (PDF) against Cryptomus — previously known as Certa Payments and Xeltox Enterprises — for processing payments that benefit the Russian government and its financial institutions. The sanctions freeze any Cryptomus assets at U.K. banks, and bar British companies from offering it any business, virtual or otherwise. Also targeted in these sanctions were the virtual payment platforms VexPay, TokenSpot, and the Tsunami Payments service.

As explained in our December 2024 investigation, “How Cryptocurrency Turns to Cash in Russian Banks,” Cryptomus has been a key payment processor for dozens of Russian cryptocurrency exchanges and websites hawking cybercrime services. The company has changed its name several times since emerging in 2022, most recently to “Heleket” last year after Canada levied a record CAD $176 million fine against the platform for anti-money laundering violations.

The blockchain data-crunching firm Chainalysis said the U.K. sanctions target the broader infrastructure supporting Russia’s wartime economy.

“Cryptomus and Heleket have emerged as major payment hubs for illicit activity,” Chainalysis wrote in an October 8 blog post. “Together, these services received more illicit funds across tracked categories than the mixers in our dataset and the number of illicit counterparties surged to more than 900 in a single month in late 2025.”

The Cryptomus support bot on Telegram, responding on Oct. 8 to an inquiry by saying the platform was suddenly unable to process transactions.

In April 2026, the blockchain analysis firm TRM Labs found Cryptomus had processed more than $7 billion in transactions since its inception in 2022, including transactions associated with child sexual abuse material vendors, terrorist financing networks, and human trafficking operations.

TRM noted that Cryptomus’s transaction volume cratered after the platform instituted new “know your customer” or KYC rules, and that the cryptocurrency platform responded to the Canadian fines by launching Heleket as a parallel service that continued to offer no-KYC payment processing.

Richard Sanders is a blockchain analyst and investigator who spent months mapping out Cryptomus’s payment infrastructure and transacting with its customers. Sanders said the U.K. sanctions on Cryptomus are important because Canada’s fine against the platform was ultimately unenforceable and uncollectible.

“A fine doesn’t stop a cryptocurrency payment processor from processing payments,” Sanders wrote on LinkedIn. “Sanctions kill the operation. The distinction matters.”

As our 2024 investigation noted, Cryptomus shared a Vancouver address with dozens of other foreign currency dealers and money transfer businesses — none of which appeared to be physically located there. Following the CAD $176 million fine for alleged money laundering violations, Cryptomus appealed the penalty, launched Heleket as a parallel service, and kept on processing large volumes of sketchy transactions, Sanders said.

Sanders said the sanctions also are notable because they are the first known instance of a “Five Eyes” country (referring to the Australia, Canada, New Zealand, the U.K. and the U.S.) sanctioning a cryptocurrency operation incorporated in another Five Eyes jurisdiction.

Where do entities like Cryptomus and Heleket derive their authority to offer international financial services? The answer appears to involve fake financial institutions overseen by a phony regulatory body positioned in a country that is not widely recognized as a sovereign nation.

Our 2024 investigation found the sole shareholder of Cryptomus’s parent company Certa Payments was a 25-year-old Ukrainian woman who also headed several other firms, including the WS Management and Advisory Corporation, which bills itself as the regulatory body that exclusively oversees licenses for cryptocurrency, foreign exchange and online gaming businesses incorporated in the disputed region in northwest Africa known as the Western Sahara.

That entity’s website — crasadr.com — says it assists applicants with bank setup and formation, online gaming licenses, and the creation and licensing of foreign exchange brokers. One of Certa Payments’ former sites — certa[.]website — also shared a server with 12 other domains, including rasd-state[.]ws, a website for the Central Reserve Authority of the Western Sahara.

The website crasadr dot com, the official website of the Central Reserve Authority of Western Sahara, which lists the entities awarded licenses for cryptocurrencies, gaming and foreign exchange services.

Feds Suspect Ransom Negotiator Ran ShinyHunters Extortion Site

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

U.S. federal investigators pursuing the ShinyHunters hacking group that recently stole highly sensitive data from the FBI have identified executives at two different ransomware negotiation firms who maintained unusually close relations with the extortion group, KrebsOnSecurity has learned. Sources close to the investigation say one of the executives in the United States even helped ShinyHunters operate a website that pressured victims into paying a ransom.

Hackers aligned with ShinyHunters have claimed credit for dozens of major data breaches since 2019, when the group was primarily operated by cybercriminals living in France. As those original members were arrested or jailed one by one, control over the ShinyHunters brand eventually passed to “Rey,” identified last year as a teenage hacker from Amman, Jordan. According to the FBI, ShinyHunters has extorted more than $70 million from victims so far this year.

In late September, ShinyHunters hacked the FBI recruitment website fbijobs.gov and taunted the agency by threatening to publish the stolen files, which reportedly included medical and psychological records, Social Security numbers and home addresses, and information about each employee’s unit and specialization.

The FBI jobs site hack has prompted the agency to move with uncommon speed to arrest, detain and question people who had any communications with Rey or other ShinyHunters suspects. Reuters reported on October 3 that Rey had been detained by Jordanian authorities and was cooperating with the FBI.

Rey was able to assume control over the hacking group because one of its alleged senior members had just been nabbed. On the evening of September 15, 2026, authorities in the Netherlands arrested convicted hacker and ShinyHunters suspect Pepijn van der Stap. Dutch police reportedly used flash-bang grenades in an effort to stun and apprehend Van der Stap as he was logged in to various online accounts.

According to a source close to the investigation, the authorities arrested Van der Stap while he was using his Signal instant messaging account, which allowed them to read recent communications with frequent contacts — including ransomware negotiators based in Canada and the United States.

Multiple sources with knowledge of the matter told KrebsOnSecurity that Van der Stap’s messages were the key to unraveling a murder-for-hire plot that emerged immediately following his arrest. The Dutch news outlet RTL reported last month the two murders were allegedly to be committed abroad, and that there are indications Van der Stap gave the order for the attacks (there is no indication those orders were ever carried out).

On October 9, KrebsOnSecurity broke the news that the FBI arrested a 54-year-old Canadian cybersecurity executive named Edward Dubrovsky in connection with the ShinyHunters investigation. Dubrovsky, who recently published a book on handling cyber extortion that recommended always engaging with the hackers, was charged with threatening to disclose stolen or hacked data and interfering with interstate commerce through extortion, according to court records. Politico reports prosecutors have asked that Dubrovsky remain in custody pending trial, and that records related to his case remain under seal.

A source close to the investigation told KrebsOnSecurity that Van der Stap’s Signal messages showed he had frequent conversations with Mr. Dubrovsky and with at least one executive at a large U.S.-based consulting firm, and that these executives often knew the details about new ShinyHunters victims before the victims themselves had even learned of the breach.

That source shared another potentially explosive finding by federal investigators: the Dutch hacker’s chats showed Mr. Dubrovsky knew ShinyHunters had stolen highly sensitive data from FBI servers, and that he never informed the bureau of the data breach prior to ShinyHunters threatening on Twitter/X to publish the agency’s deeply personal personnel files.

KrebsOnSecurity has learned from two sources close to the ShinyHunters probe that FBI investigators believe at least one executive at a U.S.-based consulting firm played an active role in operating the now-defunct ShinyHunters victim extortion website “shinywire[.]com.” KrebsOnSecurity is not naming the U.S. company or the suspected executive(s) for now because they have not yet been charged with a crime, but it is a prestigious firm with more than 1,000 associates.

A section of the homepage for the now-defunct Shinywire website.

The Shinywire website was active from approximately May 3, 2026 until October 16 and had entries for nearly 100 organizations hacked by ShinyHunters. Each entry urged victims to engage with the cybercrime group and discuss terms for payment. The pages also included samples of stolen files and promised those files would be published online if the victim ignored their demands.

The following screenshot shows a cached portion of the Shinywire extortion page for Intuitive Surgical, an American medical technology company that was breached in March 2026 after an employee fell for a targeted phishing email.

The Shinywire website, as indexed by urlscan.io. This listing is an extortion demand targeting Intuitive Surgical, an American medical technology company breached in March 2026 by a targeted phishing email attack that compromised an employee’s credentials.

Victim organizations that refused to engage or negotiate after seven days would find all of their stolen data published on the ShinyHunters victim shaming blog (a website that is only reachable via the anonymity network Tor).

In a story published October 15, The Globe and Mail wrote that a few months before Edward Dubrovsky was arrested by the FBI, the Canadian cybersecurity executive alleged in court documents he had become collateral damage in a campaign by a former business partner to “destroy” another industry executive.

The story noted that Dubrovsky filed the lawsuit in Ontario this year against Daniel Tobok, the former chief executive of Cypfer, a cybersecurity firm founded in Toronto with offices in the U.S., Britain, the Netherlands, the United Arab Emirates, and Mexico. The two first became business partners in 2017 and later developed Cypfer together, where Mr. Dubrovsky was a managing partner and chief operating officer. But by 2024, the two had a falling out, with Dubrovsky accusing Tobok of gradually excluding him from business matters and making “increasingly capricious business decisions.”

Mr. Tobok reportedly denied the allegations and filed a counterclaim, accusing Mr. Dubrovsky of accessing confidential information and unfairly competing with Cypfer through work at another firm called CyberSteward Inc. He also said Mr. Dubrovsky has misrepresented himself as a Cypfer founder, and is not a shareholder.

According to interviews with several former colleagues of Mr. Tobok, as far back as eight years ago he was known to ransomware purveyors by a particular nickname: Ghostbyte. In 2018, a Canadian company was hacked by Dharma, a prolific Russian-language ransomware affiliate program that first surfaced in 2016. The victim company told the Dharma ransomware representative they were leaning toward paying because some of the files were unrecoverable after being locked, but that they needed a better guarantee that Dharma would keep their word if paid.

A redacted copy of the Dharma rep’s reply is pictured below, and it shows the ransomware group urged the victim to contact a person at a Canadian IT firm that helps companies seeking such guarantees. The Dharma rep recommended the victim contact the email address ghostbyte@tutanota.com.

“If you want guarantees, you can write to the IT company,” the Dharma reply advised. “Take the phone and negotiate with them. This IT company often solves such problems in Canada. You can contact them and sign a contract for services. You’ll still have to pay.”

An email reply from the Dharma ransomware group to a victim, recommending they contact an email address that is connected to Mr. Tobok.

It’s unclear if Mr. Tobok ever used that Ghostbyte address offered by the Dharma representative, and he has not responded to multiple requests for comment. However, the breach tracking service Constella Intelligence finds an email address Mr. Tobok has frequently used — dtobok@me.com — is associated with two online accounts where the chosen username was “ghostbyte,” including a Snapchat account.

Incidentally, Constella also finds the Dharma rep’s email address luckygoodlucky@tuta.io is associated with a cryptocurrency address that processed 38 transactions totaling 43 bitcoin, ~$3.5 million at today’s value (the most recent transaction for this wallet is dated February 2019).

In a statement shared on October 14, Cypfer said the company’s business does not include making or facilitating ransomware payments, and that Cypfer learned of Dubrovsky’s arrest when the news first broke in the media.

“We had no prior knowledge of Mr. Dubrovsky’s arrest,” a spokesperson told KrebsOnSecurity via email. “CYPFER has not received any outreach from law enforcement and, to our knowledge, is not the target of any law enforcement investigation.”

The statement said CyberSteward and Cypfer are separate companies with no officers, directors or employees in common, and that Cypfer was recently acquired by the Apros Group, a holding company led by Jason Hogg, a former FBI special agent and former CEO of Aon Cyber Solutions Group. In 2020, Aon aquired Cytelligence, a security consulting firm where Mr. Tobok was founder and CEO and Mr. Dubrovsky was a managing partner.

It is not illegal in the United States to pay hackers for an encryption key to unlock servers infected with ransomware, or in exchange for a promise to delete any stolen files and refrain from publishing them online. While victim organizations can land in legal hot water if they pay a ransom to a group that is sanctioned by the U.S. Treasury Department, ShinyHunters is not among the ransomware groups currently sanctioned by the United States or by any other government.

Experts say ransomware victims are typically directed by their insurance provider to work with a specific incident response firm. But not all cyber forensics and breach response companies will agree to facilitate payments to ransomware groups on behalf of their clients. As a result, once victim firms make a decision to pay, the firms that eschew rewarding extortionists are invariably replaced by companies that have no such qualms.

In December 2024, ShinyHunters began extorting the education technology company PowerSchool, stealing private information on 62 million students and 9.5 million teachers in the U.S. and Canada. PowerSchool reportedly paid a $2.85 million ransom demand, and a “frequently asked questions” statement (PDF) about the breach says PowerSchool was assisted in that effort by CyberSteward (ShinyHunters affiliate Matthew Lane, 20, of Sterling, Mass., was sentenced in November 2025 to four years in prison for his admitted role in the PowerSchool hack).

But that ransom payment did not stop future extortion attempts using the same stolen data. By May 2025, individual school districts were reportedly receiving separate ransom demands using samples of the same stolen files. While nobody has alleged ShinyHunters was behind the second wave of extortion against PowerSchool users, the incident drives home the reality that there is no technical way to verify the threat actors have deleted the stolen data as promised, or prevent others from abusing the same records.

A number of leading cyber incident response firms, including Coveware and Unit 221B, have consistently warned victims against paying ShinyHunters, noting that “destroyed” victim data is often kept, resold or used to extort the company’s clients directly later on.

The FBI echoed those sentiments in an alert on May 15, 2026 advising victims against paying the ShinyHunters group. Meanwhile, ShinyHunters members have been harassing and threatening employees at Coveware and Unit 221B for offering this advice.

The following screenshots show just how annoyed the ShinyHunters leaders were with Coveware’s relentless advice against paying the group. The first image is from a conversation on May 6, 2026 that took place between the group’s main account on Telegram and a co-conspirator. In that chat, snippets of which were shared with KrebsOnSecurity by a trusted source, ShinyHunters complained that Coveware was harming the group’s payment rates.

The operator of the ShinyHunters account on Telegram speaking privately with a co-conspirator and complaining that Coveware was harming their business.

“Go on my website, majority of those companies would’ve paid if it weren’t for Coveware giving them misinformation,” the ShinyHunters account wrote. “Someone (100% Coveware) is going out of their way to make sure i am not paid.”

In another conversation on April 11, 2026, the ShinyHunters Telegram account privately said they were trying to “eliminate the problem,” which they said was Coveware. Coveware declined to comment for this story.

“I’m sure the company will pay but in this specific case I am aware of Coveware and their involvement, and I’ve probably been not getting a lot of other negotiations mostly likely because Coveware keeps getting involved,” the ShinyHunters account bemoaned.

The operator of the ShinyHunters account on Telegram telling a co-conspirator they were “trying to eliminate the problem,” which they identified as Coveware advising victims not to pay the cybercrime group.

Mark Camillo is chief executive at CyberAcuView, an industry group founded in 2021 by a consortium of major cyber insurance carriers. Camillo said some firms charge a flat retainer fee, while those that specialize in helping companies negotiate a ransom payment tend to charge a percentage of the total ransom amount paid.

That latter dynamic, he said, is an inherent conflict of interest, as the negotiator has every incentive to make things more favorable to the attackers because they are financially incentivized to negotiate and pay out, and to not negotiate down on the price.

“It has come to the industry’s attention that many of the ransomware payers charge a percentage of the final payment which may or may not be disclosed,” Camillo said. “This makes no sense if they are also the negotiator. There needs to be more transparency in the process as no one in the ecosystem should be profiting purely on the size of the ransomware transaction or payment itself.”

Camillo said the firms doing this work would argue the fees are needed because there may be unexpectedly higher costs to secure larger quantities of cryptocurrency or more risk carrying the float, and because cryptocurrency exchanges may charge higher fees for high risk transactions.

“There definitely needs to be more visibility around the transaction and clarity on the increasing value of the crypto and the exchange’s percent in the transaction to get to the bottom of this and remove any misaligned incentives,” he said.

In recent months, the U.S. Department of Justice has prosecuted a number of ransomware negotiators alleged to have conspired with cybercriminals to defraud their clients. On October 7, the DOJ charged MonsterCloud CEO Zohar Pinhasi, 50, with wire fraud and conspiracy for allegedly charging customers to remediate ransomware attacks without paying the hackers. According to charging documents, Pinhasi secretly went behind his customers’ backs and paid hackers anyway, paying ransomware groups $8 million while charging customers $19 million.

In July, Angelo Martino, a 41-year-old ransomware negotiator from Land O’Lakes, Florida, was sentenced to 70 months in prison for conspiring with the Blackcat/ALPHV ransomware group to extort multiple victims. According to the DOJ, Martino was paid by BlackCat attackers to provide confidential information about the negotiating position and strategy of his employer’s clients and enable the ransomware actors to maximize the ransoms paid by the victims.

Widespread PHP Flaw May Cause Many Site Outages Today

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Expect to see quite a few websites serving error pages today: Nearly 70 percent of websites need to update their core software to fix a pair of vulnerabilities that allow an attacker to seize full control over affected servers. Experts say there are working exploits for the flaws, and that these critical bugs are already being exploited in the wild.

The flaws at issue are present in PHP, the programming language that powers sites running on a large number of web publishing systems, including Drupal, Magento, Joomla and WordPress. Earlier today, PHP released an emergency security update to fix two critical bugs that can allow an attacker to execute code of their choosing on a vulnerable server.

Reuters reports that government websites in Europe and elsewhere went offline today after the PHP vulnerabilities were disclosed. Cloudflare said it has already detected some exploitation of the flaw, and that at 3:02 am (UTC) it implemented web application firewall rules to protect websites from attack traffic seeking to exploit the bugs, which could cause a denial of service condition, remote code execution, and data exposure.

OpenAI, which was involved in the vulnerability research and outreach, said “given the severity of these flaws and PHP’s widespread use, we consider this one of the most impactful security issues in recent memory.”

Censys, a company that tracks the technology used by Internet hosts, said a recent scan identified approximately 2.58 million Internet facing hosts and 54.9 million web properties running PHP.

Security experts at Wiz said every PHP version released in the last decade is affected by at least one of these vulnerabilities [full disclosure: Wiz is an advertiser on this blog].

“These findings rank among the most severe vulnerabilities discovered in PHP, and arguably in software history,” reads a blog post published today by a team of researchers at Wiz, which was one of several organizations credited with discovering the vulnerabilities. “The underlying flaws had been present for decades, but demonstrating their exploitability required an exceptionally complex chain of steps, at the edge of what human researchers could practically achieve. Frontier AI models substantially reduced that barrier, turning a longstanding flaw into an exploit.”

The Wiz researchers say it is likely the PHP vulnerabilities will be mass-exploited to install web-based backdoors or “web shells,” which give attackers a web-based panel that allows them to remotely control infected sites. Wiz said that prior to the PHP bugs being publicly disclosed, the company worked with the PHP team, Calif and OpenAI to measure the size of the problem and coordinate patch verification and disclosure.

The PHP zero-day vulnerabilities are being tracked by their CVE numbers, CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, and they affect all web servers running PHP 5.4 or later. The bugs are fixed in PHP 8.5.12, 8.4.27, 8.3.36, and 8.2.35.

According to Wiz, PHP is so deeply embedded in many web applications that we are likely to see organizations struggling to patch these vulnerabilities for some time to come, comparing this PHP vulnerability pair with the Log4Shell weakness that emerged in 2021 — a widespread, severe-as-it-gets security flaw in Log4j, a popular Java logging framework.

“A vulnerability in core PHP reaches far beyond a single application,” the Wiz researchers wrote. “PHP runs across clouds, hosting providers, content-delivery networks and millions of independently managed servers. Reducing exposure before the technical details became public required coordinated work across that entire ecosystem.”

The SANS Internet Storm Center is keeping an eye on major software platforms that are shipping updates to deal with these PHP flaws. For example, Ubuntu just released updated PHP packages. For a technical deep-dive on these vulnerabilities that tells the timeline of the discovery of these bugs in a comic book style, check out this post from Calif, another technology company credited with finding the PHP flaws.

A snapshot of php2shell.censys.com, the website Censys set up to track the PHP vulnerabilities.

This is a fast-moving story. Updates will be noted here with timestamps.

Update, 2:30 pm ET: Add figures from Censys and a link to Calif’s writeup.

Tech Firms Dismantle Botnet That Turned 1.3M TV Boxes Into Proxies

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Google said today it worked with industry partners to dismantle a botnet made up of more than 1.3 million Android TV set-top boxes that ship with malicious software which secretly rents the user’s Internet connection out to strangers and conducts advertising fraud. Experts involved in the takedown say the action is likely to sever the botnet’s access to more than 10 million additional compromised systems, including other Internet of Things (IoT) products such as Android-based healthcare devices and karaoke machines.

A report from the Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) said Google has severed ties with Videostrong Technology Co., a TV box manufacturer in China that GTIG and other industry experts concluded was responsible for including malware that enrolled more than 1.3 million set-top boxes in a botnet dubbed “XCPE” (named after one of the malware’s core code components).

The global distribution of XCPE botnet infections. Image: Google Threat Intelligence Group.

The report said the XCPE botnet loaded the user’s device with software that makes their Internet address available for resale by multiple “residential proxy” services, which allow customers to rent Internet addresses as a way to anonymize their web traffic or spoof their true location. As detailed in several stories here over the past year, residential proxy services are a major source of unwanted and often malicious traffic, including account takeover activity, advertising fraud, and mass content scraping for AI projects.

“Videostrong monetized the backdoor by selling access to criminal affiliates and loading those affiliates’ malware modules onto infected devices,” the GTIG post reads, noting that Google took legal action to take down the domains and servers Videostrong used to control the botnet and install additional malware. “A device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust, and represents a serious risk to those users. Without users’ knowledge or consent, infected devices were offered as a distribution mechanism for a wide array of criminal actors.”

The report notes that while TV boxes made up the the majority of the botnet, the same XCPE malware was found in other Android-based IoT products, including telehealth devices, karaoke machines, and even digital signage systems.

Google said it requires all Android device makers to pass compatibility and security tests to ensure their products don’t harm users, but that Videostrong nevertheless found a way to load their malicious software immediately after a user boots up one of these devices for the first time (Google says it has since addressed that weakness).

The GTIG report calls out a number of residential proxy providers whose software development kits were routinely downloaded by devices infected by the XCPE botnet. Those include AppSalt, also known as Honeygain, which is owned by the Vilnius, Lithuania-based Oxylabs. In October 2026, Oxylabs was relaunched under a new brand called Source, after receiving $130 million in funding from private equity firm Warburg Pincus that reportedly valued the business at $3.6 billion. Oxylabs did not respond to a request for comment.

Another provider linked to the botnet was NetNut, a once sprawling residential proxy company operated by the publicly-traded Israeli company Alarum Technologies. In July 2026, the FBI worked with industry partners to seize hundreds of domains associated with NetNut, saying the company was responsible for operating a separate botnet called Popa that enslaved TV boxes as residential proxies. Other proxy providers named in the GTIG report include EarnFM, Traffmonetizer, Jaguar, LINKFOG, BMW/IPWeb and GaGaNode.

Google’s announcement credits a number of industry partners, including Synthient, a startup that tracks proxy providers. In January 2026, Synthient published research into the rapid spread of the Kimwolf botnet, which at the time was hammering networks worldwide with record-smashing DDoS attacks. Synthient documented how Kimwolf infected millions of Android set-top boxes virtually overnight, using a widespread vulnerability in these residential proxy services to compromise other Android-based devices inside the local networks of unsuspecting TV box users.

Synthient’s research caused dozens of residential proxy providers to patch their networks, which helped to fragment the Kimwolf botnet into much smaller pieces that made its incessant and disruptive DDoS attacks far easier for network operators to manage. It also attracted the attention of the Motion Picture Association (MPA), a trade group representing the major Hollywood studios, which partnered with Synthient to purchase and tear apart a number of popular Android TV set-top boxes — including some that were being infected by Kimwolf and other IoT botnets.

In a report published today, Synthient examined a popular, pre-infected TV box sold under the brand MECOOL by a publicly traded company in China called Skyworth Technology, concluding that MECOOL also bundled the malware from Videostrong Technology Co. and likewise installed AppSalt/Honeygain and other proxy components.

The MECOOL storefront and marketing. Image: Synthient.com.

Synthient founder Ben Brundage told KrebsOnSecurity that Videostrong’s malware also distributed software for the proxy service Grass. In our November 2025 story, “Is Your Android TV Streaming Box Part of a Botnet?” we quoted a researcher from Censys who found the popular Superbox TV streaming device bundled Grass’s residential proxy software. We also quoted the Grass founder, who denied any affiliation with the TV box maker.

That November 2025 story noted that Superbox and similar Android TV set-top boxes gain new customers primarily by engaging influencers on Tiktok and other social networks who praise the product’s ability to offer hundreds of (pirated) pay TV streaming services for a one-time fee.

The popular Youtube channel Linus Tech Tips discussing MECOOL TV streaming boxes.

The GTIG report estimates the XCPE botnet takedown should sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices, noting that the various proxy and ad fraud affiliates of Videostrong “each have diversified distribution channels for their modules beyond XCPE.”

Synthient’s Brundage echoed that conclusion, calling the 1.3 million number “highly conservative.”

“The scale of affected devices is likely way higher,” he said. “This is probably the largest operation against backdoored TV boxes that we’ve seen.”

Further reading: Plume Security Labs white paper on the XCPE botnet (PDF) Black Lotus Labs writeup on LinkedIn TrendAI’s analysis Synthient’s list of affected devices, broken down by model number
