How to proof bread in the oven: Common challenge, simple solution.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

During proofing, bread and other yeasted doughs do best in a temperature-controlled, humid, draft-free environment. Your kitchen counter may not cut it (especially in colder months), but is there a corner of your kitchen that fits that description? Yes, your oven — and you don’t even need to fire it up!

Yeast dough is happiest when rising at temperatures between 75°F and 78°F. It will certainly rise in cooler or warmer temperatures. But for most doughs, that range is optimum for both yeast development and bread flavor.

Your oven’s interior will pretty much mirror room temperature. And depending on the time of year (or how much you use air conditioning), that temperature could be pretty cool—too cool for yeast’s comfort.

To get the oven up to that ideal proofing temperature, people often turn to a couple of different methods. I decided to try a bunch of them to see which is best — and helpfully, there was a clear winner.

It’s tempting to simply preheat the oven for a minute or so to warm its interior; but not only is this hit-and-miss (How long should I leave it on? Will the oven get too hot?), it’s ineffective; the heat quickly dissipates once you turn the oven off.

Enter your oven light. When turned on, your oven light’s slow, gentle heat will gradually warm the oven to the desired temperature. After trying a number of different methods for proofing bread in the oven, including several listed below, this was my clear favorite because it created the ideal temperature (not too hot, not too cold), maintained that temperature for several hours, and was essentially failproof (no overshooting the temp, for example!).

It can take your oven a while to reach the desired temperature, especially if your kitchen is cold to begin with. With so many variables — season, climate, kitchen, oven — you’ll want to do a simple experiment to determine your own best method for turning your oven into an effective, efficient proof box:

Take your oven’s temperature before turning on the light. Then turn on the light and measure the temperature again at 15-minute intervals, to see just how quickly the light warms things up. For reference, the light in my electric oven takes about an hour to raise the oven’s interior temperature by 10 degrees, and another hour to raise it an additional five degrees; your results will probably vary a bit.

Warm your oven to about 80°F before turning off the light. Over the next couple of hours, the temperature will gradually drift down by about 10 degrees, giving your rising dough plenty of time in and around that 75°F to 78°F sweet spot.

Once you know how to warm your oven using only its lightbulb, think about maintaining humidity: Dry dough can develop a tough skin, which inhibits rising.

If you’re proofing dough in a bowl (or later, a shaped loaf in a pan), cover it with a clear plastic shower cap or reusable plastic bowl cover. Whatever you use, make sure it's large enough to form a “poof” on top, so the cover doesn’t touch the rising dough.

For shaped rolls (or anything else in a large, flat pan), use an extra-large shower cap; or simply drape the pan with well-greased plastic wrap, waxed paper, or parchment.

What if your oven doesn’t have a light, the lightbulb only lights when you open the oven door, or it’s a “cool” LED bulb?

Then your best bet is a pan of boiling water set on the lowest rack, which will both warm and humidify your oven’s interior. Place the bread dough on the oven’s middle rack; no need to cover it.  Set a shallow pan (e.g., 9" x 13" pan) on the lower rack, and add four cups of boiling water. Quickly close the oven door.

Your oven’s temperature will immediately rise by about 30 degrees, which will probably be warmer than you want. Cool it down by opening the door wide for about five minutes; the temperature should drop about 15 degrees.

As the water in the oven cools, the air around it cools, too. But it happens so gradually that your oven should still remain sufficiently warm for the one to two hours your bread typically needs to rise. For longer-rising breads, simply switch out the cool water with another pan of hot water, if necessary.

Some ovens actually have a proof setting or proofing drawer. I don’t always love using this setting, though: Most are programmed to temperatures ranging from 90°F to 100°F, far warmer than yeast dough’s optimal 75°F to 78°F. For best results, figure out how long it takes to reach about 80°F, then turn it off.

Tip #1: If you have to open the oven door to check your rising dough, don’t worry. Unlike the huge 15- to 20-degree drop you experience when opening a fully heated oven, the temperature inside your light- or steam-warmed oven will only drop by a degree or two.

Tip #2: Typically, you don’t preheat your oven with rising dough inside. So when your dough is close to fully risen, remove it from the oven and set it somewhere safe. (If there’s room, the top of your fridge is both warm and out of harm’s way.) Timed just right, your bread will be perfectly proved by the time the oven finishes preheating.

Tip #3: To prevent someone from killing your rising dough by turning on the oven (say, to warm their leftover pizza), write “NO!” on a sticky note and affix it to the oven’s start button. Really!

For the highly passionate bread baker (or a small baking business), an electronic proof box like the Brod & Taylor Proofer Pro is a step up from proofing dough in the oven. It controls temperature with exquisite precision, from cold-proofing (down to 41°F) for those overnight ferments up to 122°F for an express warmup, all while ensuring perfect humidity. Wi-Fi and programmable timers let you control your dough’s development for up to eight days. It’s literally set it and forget it — the process is simple, but the results are simply amazing.

Still looking for a warm, draft-free spot for your rising bread dough? Think beyond your oven to other major appliances: Both your clothes dryer and dishwasher can double as a proof box!

The secret to make-ahead pie crust? Pie crumbs.: Get ahead on your pie baking right now.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

'Tis the season — to start thinking about holiday baking. And while this time of year is full of joy and celebration, it can also be stressful. We’re all searching for ways to get ahead of the curve, and one of the easiest? Make-ahead pie crust, made using pie crumbs.

I first learned of this technique from a 90-something friend with whom I love to talk baking. Over lunch one day, something she said about pie captured my attention.

"Did you ever hear of pie crumbs?" she asked. I admitted I hadn't — aside from the obvious: the scraps of pie crust left on the plate after the slice is gone. "My mother used to mix flour and salt and lard together to make this crumbly mixture that she'd store in a crock in the cellar. She called them her pie crumbs. When she wanted to make a pie, she'd just scoop out a few handfuls, add water, and roll out her crust."

Sounds like a good make-ahead solution for 100 years ago — but now that we have easy access to freezers, why not just make pie dough and freeze it in ready-to-roll disks? A couple of reasons, actually.

1) Pie crumbs allow you to make any amount of crust, from mini apple pies to a crust for a deep-dish 10" pie. With frozen disks, you're looking at a standard single crust — not always exactly what you want.

2) Pie crumbs save time. Remove however much of the crumbly mixture you need (more on this below) from the freezer, and within 15 minutes the fat is soft enough that you can add water and roll out your crust. Compare that to the hours it takes to thaw and roll a frozen disk of prepared crust — not to mention the elbow grease if your crust isn’t fully thawed.

3) Pie crumbs can be more stable in the freezer than pie crust disks. Why? Because without the added water, no ice crystals form. Ice crystals can damage the flour/water matrix, leading to changes in its structure (which can then potentially lead to changes in your pie crust).

Oh, and one other reason I like pie crumbs: If my friend's mother used them, they were probably a 19th-century kitchen time-saver. And I love the feeling of preserving and carrying forward our American baking traditions — even something as simple as a shortcut for pie crust.

Choose a standard pie crust recipe: Our Classic Double Pie Crust recipe is good choice, but anything that involves cutting fat into flour (lard, shortening, butter, etc.) will work.

Mix flour, salt, and shortening and/or butter: Follow your recipe up until all of the fat is incorporated into the flour. You should be left with a bowl of dry, crumbly dough that barely holds together when squeezed. If using butter, you should be able to see visible chunks scattered throughout the dough.

Weigh the pie crumbs: If you’re making a large batch of pie crumbs, it’s helpful to weigh and label the total gram amount so that you can easily remove the amount of pie crumbs for a single pie crust as needed. Our Classic Double Pie Crust makes 488 grams of pie crumbs; that means 244 grams per single pie crust. Label these amounts on your freezer storage bag.

Bag, label, and freeze the crumbs: Seal pie crumbs in an airtight, freezer-safe bag and place in the freezer, preferably in the back where it won't be subject to warm air wafting over it each time you open the freezer door.

Scoop crumbs out of the bag into a bowl: Whether one crust, two crusts, or one and a half, do the math and measure the crumbs based on your recorded gram amounts.

Let the crumbs thaw for 15 to 20 minutes or so: Wait until the butter chunks feel barely pliable in your fingers.

Add water and mix: Add water based on your original recipe amount, or just enough water to make a chunky mass when mixed. Squeeze and knead the dough briefly; all you're doing here is bringing the chunks together into one solid piece of dough.

Roll the dough, place in a pan, and bake accordingly: From here, your pie crust is the same as any fresh-made crust and can be filled and baked or prebaked for a no-bake filling. (Bonus: Because the crumbs were frozen and remain cold as you work with them, there's no need to chill your rolled-out crust before filling and/or baking.)

Fairly indefinitely, though the longer they're frozen, the more chance they have both to absorb freezer odors, and for the fats to start to go off. I've also found that, after about two weeks in the freezer, I need to add a touch more water than usual to bring the dough together — due to the drying effects inherent in freezing.

With pie season hard upon us, now's the time to try pie crumbs for make-ahead pie crust. Or maybe you have another old-fashioned pie trick up your sleeve? Share your favorite tips in the comments, below.

For detailed ingredient amounts for the crust demonstrated above, see our recipe for Classic Double Pie Crust.

And if you're looking for additional time-saving ways to use your freezer in the run-up to the holidays, check out our recipe for Homemade Brown and Serve Rolls and Apple Pie with Make-Ahead Filling.

Evaporated vs. condensed milk: What's the difference? : Navigating the canned dairy aisle.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Navigating the dairy section at the grocery store is one thing, but when it comes to the canned stuff, how much do you know about the difference between evaporated milk and condensed milk?

Before we discuss what sets evaporated milk apart from condensed milk, it’s important to discuss their similarities. Evaporated milk and condensed milk are both concentrated liquids made from fresh milk that gets heat-treated until about 60% of the water content is removed. The primary difference between the two is the amount of sugar they contain and their resulting texture.

Evaporated milk will typically only contain lactose, or the naturally occurring sugars found in milk and dairy products. Condensed milk, on the other hand, contains around 40 to 45% added sugar. That contrast means condensed milk is much thicker (almost syrupy) and has a more pronounced sweet flavor (it’s no wonder it’s often referred to as sweetened condensed milk!), and evaporated milk runs watery in texture and is lighter in taste.

Because of the extreme differences in the sugar content and consistency, evaporated milk and condensed milk cannot be used interchangeably. There will be a very noticeable difference if you use evaporated milk in a recipe that was written to use condensed milk, or vice versa.

Using evaporated in a recipe that calls for condensed milk will result in a dessert that is too bland and watery, while using condensed milk in a recipe that calls for evaporated milk can make a dish way too sweet and thick. If your recipe calls for a very small amount (1 to 2 tablespoons) of condensed milk, you could get away with combining a spoonful of evaporated milk mixed with some sugar. But if your recipe needs one whole can of evaporated or condensed milk, be sure to use the correct type.

If you want to learn more about – and celebrate – the differences between evaporated and condensed milk, make this Tres Leches Cake, which is a fantastic recipe that utilizes the two milks with heavy cream in a luscious cake soak for dessert. For a different dose of double the milk, you’ll want to try this creamy, custardy Vanilla Bean Flan.

Evaporated milk provides richness and creaminess to dishes that are both sweet and savory. It’s often used to enrich custard-based pie fillings, like Hong Kong Egg Tarts, Delicata Squash Pie, and Pumpkin Pie. To round out your fall table and use up that can of evaporated milk, try the dairy ingredient in dinner recipes like Pumpkin Soup and Creamy Tomato Soup, or macaroni and cheese.

If you have an unopened can of condensed milk, you can also make dulce de leche with it. One way to go about it is to let it simmer in a pan filled with water for a few hours: Learn how to make it here.

Is sharenting safe? 7 ways to mindfully post about your kids online

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

Your kid loses their first tooth or says something hilarious at dinner, and your thumb is already halfway to the share button. For a lot of parents and caregivers, posting these moments feels like second nature.

Believe it or not, there's actually a name for this habit: sharenting. And while it’s often a simple way to stay connected, it also puts your child’s story online before they can choose to share it. If you’ve started to wonder about the risks, it’s a good time to take stock of your posting habits.

Here's a look at why parents share and what’s worth knowing about the risks, plus seven small shifts to help you post with confidence.

Sharenting is when parents or caregivers post about their kids on social media. It could be a single birthday photo, or years of posts documenting a whole childhood. Think videos, funny quotes, school updates, potty-training wins, and even ultrasound pictures from before the baby arrives.

It's also widespread. Research reviews estimate that about three in four parents share photos, stories, or videos of their kids online, and most use their children's real names.

Parents have always shared news about their kids through holiday letters, birth announcements, and photos mailed to relatives. Social media just made the audience much bigger, the posts more public, and the record harder to remove.

Most sharenting comes from a good place. You're proud of your kid and you want the people in your life to know. Studies on parents who post about their kids point to the same reasons:

Staying close: Photos and videos let faraway family and friends watch your child grow.

Celebrating and remembering: Posts mark your child’s milestones and give you something to scroll back through later.

Finding community: Other parents and caregivers can offer advice and a “me too” at 2am, when parenting feels isolating.

Feeling seen: A kind comment on a rough day reminds you that you’re doing okay.

💙 Feeling alone as a parent? Here’s how to deal with loneliness.

Sharing your kids online isn’t necessarily harmful. Still, a few risks are worth knowing about:

A lasting digital footprint. Screenshots, reshares, and archives mean a photo can outlast the original post. Many kids have years of photos online before they're old enough to understand who can see them.

Privacy and consent. Young kids can't weigh in on what you post about them. Their public image takes shape long before they can agree to it, and that can be hard to undo later.

Personal information. Someone could piece together a full name, birth date, hometown, and school from a handful of posts. Over time, details like these may be misused for identity theft.

Location and background details. Location tags and details like a house number, school logo, or regular park in the background show where your child spends their time. On a public account, that can make their routine visible to strangers.

Facial recognition and AI. Photos on public accounts may be saved, scanned, or collected by AI tools and facial recognition software. Some of those tools turn ordinary photos into fake images.

💙 Want to bring more intention to parenting, online and off? Start here.

You don't have to log off to keep your kid safe. A few small shifts protect their privacy and still let you share the moments you love. Start with one or two of these, whatever feels most doable, then add more as they become habit.

Before you hit share, take a breath and think about whether you want to post this moment. If you do, scan the photo and caption for anything that gives away too much, like a full name or a street sign.

For some structure, a short breathing exercise or meditation can help.

💙 Listen to Pause to Breathe with Prof. Megan Reitz on Calm.

A couple of settings are worth checking before your next post. If your account is public, anyone can find and save your posts. Photos taken on your phone may also include the location where you took them.

Switch to a private account and remove followers you don’t know.

Turn off geotagging in your camera and app settings.

Platforms can change their defaults without warning, so it’s worth redoing this check every couple of months.

You can share the moment without giving away your child’s identity. Skip birth dates, school names, and daily routines. Use a nickname or first initial instead of your child’s full name.

Posting a day or two later makes it harder for anyone to know where your child is in real time.

Once your child is old enough to weigh in, show them the post and ask if they're okay with you sharing it. This shows them that their opinion matters. If your child is too young to decide, the next tip can help you decide for them.

Imagine your child at 25, seeing what you’re about to post. Does it give away something private about them, such as a health issue or a hard time at school? What seems relatable now may feel too personal to them later.

If you're unsure, keep the more personal moments in a private family group or an offline album.

💙 Explore Shift Your Perspective with Jay Shetty on Calm.

Your past posts felt right at the time, and it’s okay if some show more than you’d share today. Scroll back through your posts for details like full names, school names, or locations.

Delete the post, archive it, or move it to a private album if it feels too revealing.

You're not the only one with a camera. Grandparents, co-parents, babysitters, and friends may post photos of your child too, usually out of love and pride. A conversation up front about family boundaries keeps everyone on the same page.

leave full names and birth dates out of captions

skip location tags and recognizable landmarks in the background

use private groups or shared albums instead of public posts

💙 Calm’s Kind Communication session can help you find the words for a warm, open conversation.

Sharenting means posting about your child on social media, and the word blends "sharing" and "parenting." It covers anything from baby photos to regular updates as they grow up.

Common sharenting examples include ultrasound pictures, birthday posts, funny quotes, and health or milestone updates. Even a photo that seems harmless can reveal more than you intended, like a club sign or a team jersey in the background.

Many parents share to stay connected and celebrate their kids. Sharenting keeps faraway family members in the loop and connects you with other parents who get it. During hard stretches, it can help you feel less alone.

The main risks of sharenting include a lasting digital footprint and exposed personal details. Photos can be saved, reshared, or collected by AI tools, which is why child safety groups encourage private accounts.

That said, many posts don’t lead to harm, and small habits like turning off location tags help lower the risks further.

Sharenting shapes your child’s public image before they’re old enough to agree to it. Names, schools, and tagged locations can also reveal their routines to strangers, and a handful of details may be used for identity theft.

If you keep personal moments offline now, your child has more room to shape their own image as they grow.

There's no set age for when kids should have a say in what you post. Young kids may not fully understand what sharing means, so the decision starts with you. You can start asking as soon as they’re able to weigh in, and give their yes or no more weight as they grow.

You can practice responsible sharenting with a mindful pause before you post. From there, check your privacy settings, and leave out personal details including names, schools, and locations. Ask for your child’s input once they’re old enough, and let family members know your boundaries.

128 graduation wishes and messages for every graduate

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Graduation is so much more than a diploma. It’s often the beginning of a big, uncertain shift into the unknowns of adulthood, a new career, and a life that’s far less defined than it was when you were a student. For some grads, this shift is an exciting one. For others, it’s terrifying. And many feel both.

If someone you love is graduating, you may want to write them a message that honors this journey. You want your note to feel personal, thoughtful, and maybe even a little inspiring — but finding the right words isn’t always easy.

The good news is you don’t need to be poetic or profound to share a meaningful sentiment on your loved one’s special day. A simple, warm message that acknowledges the moment and the person is more than enough. Whether you’re writing to a high school senior, a college grad, or someone finishing their doctorate, we’ve pulled together graduation wishes that are thoughtful, grounded, and easy to make your own.

The best wishes are the ones that make the graduate feel seen and loved. You recognize all the effort they’ve put in and offer encouragement without adding expectations.

Here are four aspects of a great graduation wish:

It’s genuine: A heartfelt “I’m proud of you” means more than a forced life lesson.

It reflects your relationship: Speak the way you typically would with them. If you’re usually lighthearted, stay lighthearted. If you’re more heartfelt and earnest, go that route.

It acknowledges the transition: Graduation is a moment of change, which can be both exciting and uncertain. Acknowledging this can help bring relief to the graduate.

It avoids pressure: Avoid mapping out their future or telling them what’s next, as this can add pressure. Instead, aim for something like, “Whatever comes next, I believe you’ll succeed.”

Graduation cards can have a tendency to lean on phrases like “Wishing you all the best!” and “The future is yours!” On the surface level, these messages can be sweet, but they don’t always reflect the depth of the relationship you’ve built.

If you want the graduate in your life to feel seen by you, here are 72 wishes that can help you out.

Watching your child graduate may leave you feeling pretty emotional. Here are eight ways to honor their accomplishment and the person they’ve become:

Seeing you graduate makes me beam. I’m so proud of you.

Your hard work and determination have paid off big time. Let’s celebrate!

I’m so proud of the person you’ve become (and have always been).

This achievement is just the beginning. Can’t wait to see all that you do!

Every day you make us proud, but today especially. Congratulations!

Your journey has been inspiring. I’m honored to be your parent.

You graduated! Let’s celebrate this huge milestone. You earned it and then some.

Your future is bright, and I can’t wait to see it unfold.

Siblings often see the late-night essay writing and the quiet doubts their siblings experience. To reflect that, here are eight wishes that are both playful and heartfelt:

I’ve seen every late-night study session and all the hair-pulling that got you here. You earned this.

No one works harder than you, and look at what it led to. Congrats!

Watching you grow into yourself has been the best part of being your sibling. (Now, could you stop growing? You’re making me look bad.)

So proud to call you my sibling and even prouder to celebrate this with you.

You’re my favorite hustler. And you did it all without losing who you are. Congratulations!

Thanks for graduating so Mom and Dad can focus on you for a bit, and I can act out without them noticing.

Congratulations to the second-smartest kid in the family. (Just kidding!) I love you and I’m so proud of you.

I hope you’re taking a second to soak it all in. You deserve it. (Okay, that’s enough gushing. As your sibling, it’s my job to keep your ego in check!)

Your friends have seen you at your best and worst and everything in between. Here are eight ways to reflect that journey with warmth and honesty:

You finally graduated! Now, can we finally book a trip to celebrate in style?

You can get a full night’s rest now. Congratulations!

Hope you’re ready for me to be very obnoxious and introduce you as “doctor” from now on.

Start stretching now, because this is huge, and we’re going to dance until morning to celebrate it properly!

I’m lucky to call you a friend. Congratulations. I’m beyond proud.

You’re the hardest worker I know. Congrats. You deserve it!

Get ready to be embarrassed: Nobody is going to have a louder cheering section at graduation than you. Love you!

You’re so impressive, and I learn so much from you all the time. Congratulations, friend!

If you’re a cousin, grandparent, aunt, or uncle, here are eight wishes that you can show support:

I’ve seen all the behind-the-scenes work that went into this moment. You’ve earned every bit of it.

You are so strong and resilient. I had no doubt this day would come. Congratulations!

You’ve grown into someone who leads with compassion and courage. It’s been a privilege to witness that.

This moment belongs to you. Now, let’s celebrate!

Congratulations on a job well done. “Proud” is an understatement.

Your dedication and heart are incredible to witness. Thanks for showing us how it’s done. Congrats!

I’m standing up a little taller today because you’re a part of our family. Congratulations.

Your tenacity and kindness are an inspiration. We love you and congrats!

What a joy to watch your grandchild graduate. Here are eight wishes that emphasize your love, pride, and admiration:

Watching you hit this milestone has made me so happy. Can’t wait to see all the other ones you reach.

You make this family proud every single day, but today especially. Congratulations!

This diploma is just the beginning. I love you and congratulations.

You’ve made your grandparents grin from ear to ear. Congratulations.

You did something incredible, and we’ve loved every moment of cheering you on.

I’m so happy I got to witness this day. You’re very special, and you make the world a better place.

Congratulations. But I’m not surprised at all. I’ve seen your work ethic, and I’ve seen your heart. This is just the start.

May this achievement remind you how deeply you’re loved, and of how deeply proud we are of you every single day.

Here are eight ways to say “I see you” to your partner and celebrate their big win:

Every day I stare at you in awe, but today even more so. Congratulations, I love you.

I saw firsthand how hard you worked for this. No one deserves this more than you.

You’ve earned every bit of this joy, and I’m so lucky to share it with you.

Hey, smartypants. Congrats! I love you, and I can’t believe I get to share my life with you.

You never needed a diploma to impress me. You’ve already done that just by being you (and with your killer back rubs).

The world is your oyster, and I’m so happy to be in the sea near you, watching you thrive.

You’ve built something incredible. I hope today you let yourself really feel that. Tonight, we’re celebrating!

This is just one of many moments I’m proud to be your partner. You’re the coolest, plain and simple.

Each type of graduation can bring its own set of emotions. It can bring excitement at a first leap into independence, anxiety at what’s next, or joy at crossing a finish line that took years to reach.

To help tailor your wishes to the specific stage, here are 56 thoughtful messages you can use.

Good wishes for high school graduates should be grounded in encouragement. This is a time when everything feels new and a little uncertain. The best messages usually strike a balance between celebrating a significant achievement and acknowledging the emotional weight of leaving behind the familiar.

You might say something like: “You just accomplished something huge. Congratulations! And remember, it’s more than okay if the next step still feels a little uncertain.” This way, you’re honoring their hard work but also reminding them that they don’t need to have everything figured out yet.

Start by acknowledging the journey they’ve been on. College is typically full of academic pressure and personal growth. Let them know how proud you are of the person they’ve become.

You might say something like, “You’ve worked so hard, and I can’t wait to see what comes next. I’m so proud of the incredible person you are.”

Congratulate them, then add one kind thing about them. A few lines is plenty for a card. Here are some words for a graduation card, from short greetings to more personal notes:

"Congrats, grad! Your future is lucky to have you."

"Congratulations, graduate! I'm so proud of you."

"I've seen how much effort this took. I hope you feel so proud today."

"You kept going, even when things were hard. That says so much about who you are."

A good way to personalize a message is to reflect on what you admire about the graduate or how you’ve seen them evolve. Try naming something specific, like a moment they overcame something, or how they’ve impacted you.

Remember, you don’t need to write a long note. A short message like, “You’ve always led with compassion, and I admire that so much,” is powerful.

Funny graduation wishes are okay — just make sure the tone matches the moment. A light joke about student loans, caffeine dependency, or group projects gone wrong can land if it feels warm and comes from a place of love.

But as a rule of thumb, aim to avoid anything that pokes fun at their future or compares them to others.

A short message can still carry a lot of heart if it’s honest and specific. Here are a few examples that speak to a person’s strength and growth.

“You did it even when it felt impossible, and that says everything about how strong you are.”

“You poured your sweat and tears into this and earned every moment of it. I’m beyond proud.”

“I couldn’t be prouder of you and can’t wait to see what you do next.”

A good congratulations message for a graduate does two things. It says congratulations in plain words, and it names one thing you admire, like how hard they worked.

How you deliver it matters too. In person, a hug and “I’m so proud of you” mean a lot. In a card or text, you might write, “Congratulations on your graduation! I’ve loved watching you work toward this day.” Skip questions about what comes next. Many grads are still figuring that out, and today is for celebrating what they’ve done.

Start with congratulations, then add one thing you've loved watching her do. A line or two is plenty for a card. Here are short graduation messages for a granddaughter, whether she's finishing high school, college, or another program:

"So proud of you, my granddaughter. Congratulations on your graduation!"

"Watching you become who you are has been one of the great joys of my life. Congratulations, sweetheart."

"You're a very special young woman, and I'm so proud of all you've worked for. Congratulations!"

"A college degree! Granddaughter, you've made me so proud. Congratulations."

"Being your grandma has been one of the best parts of my life. Congratulations, graduate!"

Congratulate the achievement and acknowledge the time it took. A doctorate often means years of research and writing, so mentioning that effort shows you see it. A line or two is enough. Here are a few short messages for a PhD or doctorate degree:

"Congratulations on completing your PhD. I've loved watching you work toward this day."

"You earned this doctorate through years of hard work. So proud of you!"

"Congratulations on your doctorate degree. I hope you take time to enjoy this one."

How to stop emotional flooding from disrupting your relationships

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

The argument started with the dishes. Now you're saying something you know you’ll regret. Two minutes ago, you were mildly annoyed. Now your heart is pounding, and you can barely hear your partner over the noise in your own head.

This is emotional flooding, and it can happen to anyone. It isn’t a character flaw, and it doesn’t mean you're bad at relationships. When emotions run hot, the level-headed version of you can seem to vanish.

Flooding tends to show up when you're close to someone and the stakes feel personal. Fortunately, if you spot the signs early, you can keep an argument from turning into a blowup. Ahead, we’ll explore the science behind emotional flooding and how to calm yourself when it hits.

Emotional flooding is a state of overwhelm during a heated moment, usually a conflict. The term comes from John Gottman, PhD, who describes flooding as the point when you’re so overloaded that you can't take in new information, solve the problem, or hear the person in front of you.

Gottman and his colleague Robert Levenson studied couples at the University of Washington. As partners argued, they tracked physical signals like heart rate. Couples whose bodies showed higher arousal during conflict tended to grow less satisfied with their relationship over the following years.

Flooding feels different from person to person, so you may notice some of these signs but not others. Here's what to look for:

Racing heart: Your pulse climbs, and you feel your heartbeat in your chest or throat.

Fast, shallow breathing: Your breaths come quick and tight, and a full inhale feels out of reach.

Heat and tension: Your face flushes, and your jaw or shoulders tighten.

Tunnel vision: Your thinking narrows, and the other person’s words get hard to follow.

Urge to fight or flee: You want to raise your voice or leave, and it feels difficult to hold back.

Numbness or blankness: Your thoughts freeze, and you feel disconnected from the conversation.

Emotional flooding involves the amygdala, the part of your brain built to detect danger and keep you safe. When it senses a threat, it triggers the stress response and releases a surge of hormones that put your body on high alert. Adrenaline and norepinephrine speed up your heart rate and pull your focus toward the threat. That narrowed focus is part of why listening gets so hard.

In an everyday disagreement, there’s usually no physical danger, but your brain can still treat a raised voice or a difference of opinion as a threat, setting off the same alarm and the same rush of anger.

In close relationships, flooding may get extra fuel. Arguments can touch things that matter deeply to you, like feeling respected or safe. Old wounds and fear of where the fight might lead can pile on top of the present moment.

A note on relationships that don’t feel safe: This article is about everyday arguments, not abuse. If a raised voice or a pattern of control leaves you afraid of your partner, that fear isn’t a false alarm. Self-soothing isn’t the tool for this, and your safety comes first. You can reach the National Domestic Violence Hotline any time at 1-800-799-7233, or text START to 88788.

You can't always stop flooding before it starts, but you can help your body calm down once you realize it’s happening. Then, when you feel settled enough to think clearly again, you can gently return to the conversation. The tips below follow that order: calm your body first, then come back and repair. Try the ones that fit the moment, and skip any that don’t feel right for you.

The moment you notice your heart racing or your thoughts scrambling, tell yourself “this is flooding.” Naming it reminds you that the feeling is physical and temporary. That bit of awareness gives you room to choose your next move instead of reacting on autopilot.

💙 Try the Full Sensory Awareness meditation on the Calm app.

A longer exhale engages your parasympathetic nervous system, the part of your nervous system that helps you rest and settle down.

In one study, people who practiced cyclic sighing (also known as the physiological sigh), a breathing pattern of two quick inhales and one long exhale, reported bigger mood improvements than those who practiced mindfulness meditation.

If breathing exercises feel uncomfortable, skip ahead to the next tip.

💙 Press play on the Breathe to Calm Down session on Calm.

When you're flooded, stepping away can help you collect yourself before you talk things through. Tell your partner you need a break and that you’ll come back, so they don’t feel abandoned mid-conflict.

According to Gottman's research, it can take at least 20 minutes for your body to calm back down, so give yourself enough time. If you can’t leave the room, even a few minutes of silence can help.

Your body often responds to physical cues faster than logic. Use your time-out to splash cold water on your face or step outside for a few minutes of fresh air. Either one can help slow your heart rate.

💙 If anger is running hot, listen to Calm’s Investigating Anger session.

Flooding releases stress hormones that prepare your body for action. A brisk walk, or any gentle movement that suits you, gives that energy somewhere to go instead of letting it build.

💙 Explore Dr. Eric López's Walk Away Stress series on Calm.

Once it feels calmer, return to your partner. You don't have to solve everything right away. A short repair can open the door to trying again.

Try: “I got overwhelmed earlier. Can we try again?”

Gottman’s work suggests that how couples repair after conflict is a key predictor of long-term satisfaction, often more telling than how often they fight.

💙 Learn how couples meditation can strengthen your relationship.

Emotional flooding is feeling so overwhelmed during a conflict or another intense moment that you can’t think clearly or listen. The term comes from psychologist John Gottman's research on couples.

A common example of emotional flooding is a tense meeting with your boss, when your heart starts pounding and your mind goes blank. It can also happen at home, like when a conversation with a family member or partner escalates.

💙 Check out how to deal with a conflict at work.

Emotional flooding is a surge of intense emotion in a specific moment, while emotional dysregulation is ongoing difficulty managing emotions across a range of situations. Flooding can be one sign of dysregulation, but if it happens now and then, that doesn’t necessarily mean you have a pattern of dysregulation.

Emotional flooding can be more common and more intense for people with ADHD (attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder). Studies show that trouble managing emotions is a core part of ADHD in adults. With ADHD, you may flood more often or take longer to calm down.

If flooding feels constant or unmanageable, a doctor can help you figure out what’s behind it.

💙 Learn what ADHD shutdown is and how to manage it.

You can stop being emotionally flooded by giving your body time to settle before you continue the conversation. Take a break of at least 20 minutes, and try long exhales, cold water on your face, or a brisk walk. When you feel more grounded, give the conversation another try.

Mindfulness can help with emotional flooding because it trains you to notice early signs, such as a racing heart or a tight jaw, before they build. Over time, that gives you a chance to pause instead of react in hard moments.

Try to practice mindfulness when you’re calm, with short breathing exercises or guided meditation, so the skill is there when you need it.

Mental health is hard. Getting support doesn't have to be. The Calm app puts the tools to feel better in your back pocket, with personalized content to manage stress and anxiety, get better sleep, and feel more present in your life.
